You can adapt this for Colab Enterprise or run it locally. Gradio often just works in standard Colab by displaying in the output cell. On Colab Enterprise, you may need to open the Gradio interface via a public URL if direct inline display is blocked.
1. Prepare Your Data

Assume you have:

    A folder word_crops/ containing images: word_0.png, word_1.png, etc.

    A CSV, e.g. recognized_words.csv, with two columns:

    crop_filename,recognized_text
    word_0.png,HELLO
    word_1.png,WROL
    word_2.png,THS
    word_3.png,IS
    ...

You’ll load this CSV into Python, store it in a list (or dict), and let the user correct each text label.
2. Install Gradio

!pip install gradio

In a local Python environment or Colab. If you’re on Colab Enterprise, also ensure your organization’s policies permit installing extra packages.
3. Create and Run the Script

Below is a self-contained Python script using Gradio. In standard Colab, you can just paste this into a cell. If you’re local, place it in a .py file (e.g., labeling_app.py) and run with python labeling_app.py.

import gradio as gr
import csv
import os
from PIL import Image

# ---------------------------
# 1) Load the dataset
# ---------------------------
CSV_FILE = "recognized_words.csv"   # Adjust path if needed
IMAGE_FOLDER = "word_crops"         # Folder with cropped word images

samples = []  # Will hold a list of dicts: {"filename": ..., "label": ...}

with open(CSV_FILE, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as f:
    reader = csv.DictReader(f)
    for row in reader:
        samples.append({
            "filename": row["crop_filename"],
            "label": row["recognized_text"]
        })

# We'll keep an index in a Gradio "state" to track which sample we're viewing
# and store the entire dataset in memory.

# ---------------------------
# 2) Helper functions
# ---------------------------
def load_sample(idx):
    """
    Given an index, return (image, label) for that sample.
    We'll load the image from disk and return it as a PIL Image.
    """
    # Clamp index in case user goes past boundaries
    idx = max(0, min(len(samples) - 1, idx))
    sample = samples[idx]

    # Construct full path to the image
    img_path = os.path.join(IMAGE_FOLDER, os.path.basename(sample["filename"]))

    # Load image
    if os.path.exists(img_path):
        pil_img = Image.open(img_path)
    else:
        # If not found, generate a placeholder or raise an error
        pil_img = Image.new("RGB", (300, 100), color="red")

    # Return the image object + the recognized/corrected label + the current index
    return pil_img, sample["label"], idx

def navigate(current_label, idx, direction):
    """
    Store the current_label in samples[idx], then move the index
    forward (direction=1) or backward (direction=-1).
    Return the updated (image, label, new_idx) for display.
    """
    # Update the in-memory label
    samples[idx]["label"] = current_label

    # Move index
    new_idx = idx + direction
    new_idx = max(0, min(len(samples) - 1, new_idx))

    # Load sample at new_idx
    img, lbl, new_idx = load_sample(new_idx)
    return img, lbl, new_idx

def save_csv():
    """
    Write all updated labels back to CSV_FILE.
    """
    with open(CSV_FILE, 'w', newline='', encoding='utf-8') as f:
        writer = csv.writer(f)
        writer.writerow(["crop_filename", "recognized_text"])
        for s in samples:
            writer.writerow([s["filename"], s["label"]])
    return "CSV saved successfully!"

# ---------------------------
# 3) Build Gradio Interface
# ---------------------------
def on_start():
    # This function will load the 0th sample on startup
    return load_sample(0)  # (image, label, index=0)

with gr.Blocks() as demo:
    # We'll store the current index in a Gradio state
    state_idx = gr.State(value=0)

    # Title / instructions
    gr.Markdown("# Handwriting Labeling\nUse the buttons below to navigate and fix any incorrect text.")

    # Display area: an Image output, plus a Textbox for editing
    image_out = gr.Image(type="pil", label="Word Crop")
    label_box = gr.Textbox(label="Corrected Label")

    # Navigation buttons
    btn_prev = gr.Button("Previous")
    btn_next = gr.Button("Next")
    btn_save = gr.Button("Save CSV")

    # Status area
    status = gr.Label(value="")

    # When the app loads, we initialize the first sample:
    demo.load(on_start, inputs=None, outputs=[image_out, label_box, state_idx])

    # Clicking "Previous": store label, move index -1, load new sample
    btn_prev.click(
        fn=lambda current_label, idx: navigate(current_label, idx, direction=-1),
        inputs=[label_box, state_idx],
        outputs=[image_out, label_box, state_idx],
    )

    # Clicking "Next": store label, move index +1, load new sample
    btn_next.click(
        fn=lambda current_label, idx: navigate(current_label, idx, direction=1),
        inputs=[label_box, state_idx],
        outputs=[image_out, label_box, state_idx],
    )

    # Save CSV
    btn_save.click(
        fn=save_csv,
        inputs=[],
        outputs=status  # We'll display a success message here
    )

# 4) Launch the Gradio app
demo.launch()

What This Code Does

    Load your CSV lines into a samples list of dicts, each with "filename" and "label".
    on_start: On app startup, load the first sample (idx=0).
    navigate function:
        Saves the user’s current text input into samples[idx]["label"].
        Increments or decrements idx by direction.
        Returns a new (image, label, idx) to display.
    save_csv function:
        Iterates through samples and rewrites the CSV with any corrected labels.

4. Running the App

    Local / Desktop:
        Put labeling_app.py and your CSV + word_crops/ in the same folder.
        python labeling_app.py
        Gradio will print a local URL (usually http://127.0.0.1:7860). Open it in your browser.
    Colab (Standard):
        Paste the code in a cell.
        Press the Run button for the cell.
        You should see a Gradio interface appear in the output. If not, you may get a link to open in a new browser tab.
    Colab Enterprise:
        Depending on policies, you may see the interface in the output cell or a public link. If strict firewall rules block it, you might need to create a tunnel or run this script locally.

5. Tips & Customizations

    Batch Editing: Instead of one image at a time, you could show multiple images in a grid for faster labeling (though the code gets more complex).
    Skipping Some Samples: If you only want to fix uncertain or suspicious text, you can filter them out in your CSV or add a “Confidence” column.
    Jump to Index: You can add a numeric input to jump to a certain index quickly.
    Auto-Save: Instead of separate “Save CSV,” you could rewrite the CSV each time you click “Next” or “Previous,” but that can be slower for large datasets.
    Large Datasets: If you have thousands of images, break them up into smaller CSV chunks to reduce overhead.

6. Summary

This Gradio interface lets you:

    Cycle through each cropped word image.
    See and correct the recognized text.
    Save updated labels to a CSV file.

It’s more user-friendly than a spreadsheet, since each image is displayed inline. Once you’re done, your recognized_words.csv will contain the correct transcription for each image—ideal for fine-tuning your handwriting OCR models. Enjoy labeling!
You said:
Two things: Can I make this more efficient by accepting an enter keypress in the corrected label field to advance to the next word? Also, can I save the CSV to a different file so as to preserve the original input unchanged?
Reasoned for a few seconds

Below is a revised Gradio example that does two things:

    Press Enter to Advance: When you press Enter (submit) in the text box, it automatically saves the current label and advances to the next image—no need to click a “Next” button.
    Save to a Separate File: Instead of overwriting the original CSV, it writes to a new file (e.g., recognized_words_labeled.csv), preserving the original input.

You can still keep explicit “Previous” or “Next” buttons if you want them. This example removes them for simplicity—pressing enter in the text box becomes your main way to move forward. (You could also add a “Previous” button if desired.)
1. Example Code

import gradio as gr
import csv
import os
from PIL import Image

# --- 1) FILE PATHS ---
CSV_INPUT = "recognized_words.csv"                # original file (read-only)
IMAGE_FOLDER = "word_crops"                       # folder with cropped images
CSV_OUTPUT = "recognized_words_labeled.csv"       # new file to write updated labels

# --- 2) LOAD SAMPLES ---
samples = []  # will hold dicts: {"filename": ..., "label": ...}

with open(CSV_INPUT, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as f:
    reader = csv.DictReader(f)
    for row in reader:
        samples.append({
            "filename": row["crop_filename"],
            "label": row["recognized_text"]
        })

# We'll track the current index in Gradio state
def load_sample(idx):
    """Return (image, label, corrected_idx)."""
    # Clamp the index
    idx = max(0, min(len(samples) - 1, idx))
    sample = samples[idx]

    # Construct full image path
    img_path = os.path.join(IMAGE_FOLDER, os.path.basename(sample["filename"]))
    if os.path.exists(img_path):
        pil_img = Image.open(img_path)
    else:
        pil_img = Image.new("RGB", (300, 100), color="red")  # placeholder if missing

    return pil_img, sample["label"], idx

def handle_submit(current_label, idx):
    """
    Saves the current label in samples[idx], increments idx, 
    loads the next sample. (Effectively a 'Next' triggered by pressing Enter.)
    """
    # Update in-memory label
    samples[idx]["label"] = current_label
    new_idx = idx + 1
    if new_idx >= len(samples):
        new_idx = len(samples) - 1  # stay on last if user hits Enter on last item
    return load_sample(new_idx)

def save_csv():
    """
    Writes the entire updated labels to CSV_OUTPUT.
    """
    with open(CSV_OUTPUT, 'w', newline='', encoding='utf-8') as f:
        writer = csv.writer(f)
        writer.writerow(["crop_filename", "recognized_text"])
        for s in samples:
            writer.writerow([s["filename"], s["label"]])
    return f"Labels saved to {CSV_OUTPUT}."

# --- 3) BUILD GRADIO INTERFACE ---
def on_start():
    """Load the first sample on app load."""
    return load_sample(0)  # image, label, index=0

with gr.Blocks() as demo:
    gr.Markdown("# Handwriting Labeling\n**Press Enter** to save and advance to the next word.")

    state_idx = gr.State(value=0)  # track current index
    image_out = gr.Image(type="pil", label="Word Crop")
    label_box = gr.Textbox(label="Corrected Label")
    status = gr.Label()

    # Initialize the first sample when the app loads
    demo.load(on_start, inputs=None, outputs=[image_out, label_box, state_idx])

    # Pressing Enter in the text box triggers handle_submit
    label_box.submit(
        fn=handle_submit,
        inputs=[label_box, state_idx],
        outputs=[image_out, label_box, state_idx]
    )

    # A "Save CSV" button if you want to finalize to the new file
    btn_save = gr.Button("Save CSV")
    btn_save.click(
        fn=save_csv,
        inputs=[],
        outputs=status
    )

demo.launch()

Explanation

    CSV_INPUT: Points to your original recognized_words.csv. We do not overwrite it.
    CSV_OUTPUT: A new file, e.g. recognized_words_labeled.csv, where we store updated labels.
    No Next Button:
        Instead, we rely on label_box.submit(...). When you press Enter in the text box, it calls handle_submit.
        handle_submit updates the current label in memory, increments the index, and loads the next word.
    Save CSV Button:
        Writes the entire updated dataset to CSV_OUTPUT.
        You can press this any time to persist your changes.

If you need a Previous button, you could add something like:

btn_prev = gr.Button("Previous")
btn_prev.click(
    fn=lambda current_label, idx: handle_submit(current_label, idx - 2),
    inputs=[label_box, state_idx],
    outputs=[image_out, label_box, state_idx],
)

But that’s a bit hacky because handle_submit always increments. Instead, you’d define a separate function go_prev(current_label, idx) that updates the label but decrements.
2. Usage Flow

    Run this script (either locally python script.py or in Colab).
    Gradio will show an interface with the first word crop and a text box.
    Type (or correct) the text label, press Enter → the next image automatically loads.
    Repeat until done.
    Press Save CSV at any point to write changes to recognized_words_labeled.csv.

3. Summary

    Press Enter to advance is handled by the submit event on the text box.
    Preserve Original by only reading from CSV_INPUT and writing updates to a separate CSV_OUTPUT.
    This flow is typically faster than having separate Next/Previous buttons, especially if you mainly want to correct each label in one pass.

You said:
One more thing: Can I start with the labelled text field highlighted so that I can just start typing to replace the original?
Reasoned for 13 seconds

Below is an updated Gradio example where pressing Enter still advances to the next word and the text box is automatically focused (and selected) each time a new sample is loaded—so you can just start typing immediately without extra clicks.

Because Gradio doesn’t (yet) have a built-in “autoFocus” parameter, we’ll use a small HTML/JS snippet that triggers focus/selection on the text box after each update.
1. Example Code

import gradio as gr
import csv
import os
from PIL import Image

# --- FILE PATHS ---
CSV_INPUT = "recognized_words.csv"       
IMAGE_FOLDER = "word_crops"            
CSV_OUTPUT = "recognized_words_labeled.csv"  

# --- LOAD DATASET ---
samples = []  # list of {"filename": str, "label": str}
with open(CSV_INPUT, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as f:
    reader = csv.DictReader(f)
    for row in reader:
        samples.append({
            "filename": row["crop_filename"],
            "label": row["recognized_text"]
        })

def load_sample(idx):
    """Given an index, return (image, label, idx)."""
    idx = max(0, min(len(samples) - 1, idx))
    sample = samples[idx]
    img_path = os.path.join(IMAGE_FOLDER, os.path.basename(sample["filename"]))
    if os.path.exists(img_path):
        pil_img = Image.open(img_path)
    else:
        pil_img = Image.new("RGB", (300, 100), color="red")  # placeholder

    return pil_img, sample["label"], idx

def handle_submit(current_label, idx):
    """
    Save the current label, increment idx, load next sample.
    Return (image, label, new_idx, focus_script).
    """
    samples[idx]["label"] = current_label
    new_idx = idx + 1
    if new_idx >= len(samples):
        new_idx = len(samples) - 1

    # Load the new sample
    pil_img, lbl, new_idx = load_sample(new_idx)

    # Return a small JS snippet to auto-focus the text box
    focus_script = """
    <script>
    const ta = document.querySelector('textarea[id^="component-"]');
    if (ta) {
      ta.focus();
      ta.select();
    }
    </script>
    """

    return pil_img, lbl, new_idx, focus_script

def save_csv():
    """Write all updated labels to CSV_OUTPUT."""
    with open(CSV_OUTPUT, 'w', newline='', encoding='utf-8') as f:
        writer = csv.writer(f)
        writer.writerow(["crop_filename", "recognized_text"])
        for s in samples:
            writer.writerow([s["filename"], s["label"]])
    return f"Labels saved to {CSV_OUTPUT}!"

def on_start():
    """Load the first sample on app load (index=0)."""
    return load_sample(0)  # image, label, index=0

with gr.Blocks() as demo:
    gr.Markdown("""
    # Handwriting Labeling
    **Press Enter** to save the current label and move on to the next word.
    The text box will auto-focus for quick typing.
    """)

    state_idx = gr.State(value=0)
    image_out = gr.Image(type="pil", label="Word Crop")
    label_box = gr.Textbox(label="Corrected Label")
    status = gr.Label()

    # We'll use an HTML component to inject the "focus" script each time
    focus_html = gr.HTML()

    # 1) Initial load -> get first sample
    demo.load(
        fn=on_start,
        inputs=None,
        outputs=[image_out, label_box, state_idx],
        # If you also want to auto-focus on the very first load, you can set a manual HTML snippet here too
    )

    # 2) Submitting (pressing Enter in the textbox) -> handle_submit
    #    We'll also output the focus_html snippet to re-focus the box
    label_box.submit(
        fn=handle_submit,
        inputs=[label_box, state_idx],
        outputs=[image_out, label_box, state_idx, focus_html]
    )

    # 3) "Save CSV" button
    btn_save = gr.Button("Save CSV")
    btn_save.click(
        fn=save_csv,
        inputs=[],
        outputs=status
    )

demo.launch()

Explanation of the Key Changes

    focus_html Output: We define a small JavaScript snippet that:

    const ta = document.querySelector('textarea[id^="component-"]');
    if (ta) {
      ta.focus();
      ta.select();
    }

    This ensures the Gradio <textarea> gets focused and its text is selected, so you can immediately overtype.

    Returning focus_script: After each submission (label_box.submit), we return (image, label, new_idx, focus_script). Gradio automatically updates the focus_html component with that script, causing the text box to focus.

    Saving to a Separate CSV: We read from recognized_words.csv but write to recognized_words_labeled.csv, preserving the original.

    Press Enter to Advance: The label_box.submit calls handle_submit, which increments the index and loads the next sample. No “Next” button needed.

2. Usage Flow

    Run the script (locally or in Colab).
    A Gradio interface appears with the first image and text.
    Start typing immediately (the text box is focused) or press Enter to replace the existing text and move on.
    If you want to preserve part of the recognized text, you can use arrow keys or backspace—the entire text is selected when you focus, but partial editing is easy.
    When done, press Save CSV to write all changes to recognized_words_labeled.csv.

3. Further Customizations

    Add a “Previous” Button: If you occasionally need to go backward, you can add a small button that calls a function go_prev(current_label, idx) that sets idx - 1.
    Auto-Save Each Time**: Instead of a separate “Save CSV” step, you could call save_csv automatically after each submission. This might slow you down if you have thousands of samples, though.
    Keyboard Shortcut for “Save CSV”**: Gradio doesn’t natively provide custom key bindings, but you could embed additional JavaScript if you want.

With this approach, you get a fast labeling cycle: type → Enter → next image auto-focus → type → Enter. No extra clicks needed!
You said:
Hmm, the highlighting script doesn't seem to be working. Is there an alternate approach?
Reasoned for 19 seconds

Below is a streamlined Gradio snippet that:

    Loads your (image, recognized_text) pairs.
    Pressing Enter in the text box saves the current label, moves to the next image.
    Automatically focuses and selects the text in the box after each submission, so you can just start typing the correction right away.

We’ll leverage Gradio’s built-in submit(..., _js=...) parameter, which runs a small JavaScript callback after the Python function completes—this is often more reliable than injecting HTML scripts.
1. Example Code

import gradio as gr
import csv
import os
from PIL import Image

# --- FILE PATHS ---
CSV_INPUT = "recognized_words.csv"           # original input (read only)
CSV_OUTPUT = "recognized_words_labeled.csv"  # corrected labels go here
IMAGE_FOLDER = "word_crops"

# --- LOAD DATASET ---
samples = []  # will hold dicts: {"filename": str, "label": str}
with open(CSV_INPUT, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as f:
    reader = csv.DictReader(f)
    for row in reader:
        samples.append({
            "filename": row["crop_filename"],
            "label": row["recognized_text"]
        })

# We'll keep an integer index in Gradio's State
def load_sample(idx):
    """Return (image, label, valid_idx)."""
    # Clamp idx
    idx = max(0, min(len(samples) - 1, idx))
    sample = samples[idx]
    img_path = os.path.join(IMAGE_FOLDER, os.path.basename(sample["filename"]))
    if os.path.exists(img_path):
        pil_img = Image.open(img_path)
    else:
        pil_img = Image.new("RGB", (300,100), color="red")  # fallback
    return pil_img, sample["label"], idx

def handle_submit(current_label, idx):
    """
    Save the current label to samples[idx],
    increment idx, and load the next sample.
    """
    # Save the newly typed label
    samples[idx]["label"] = current_label

    # Move to the next
    new_idx = idx + 1
    if new_idx >= len(samples):
        new_idx = len(samples) - 1  # stay on last if we exceed

    # Load next sample
    return load_sample(new_idx)  # (image, label, new_idx)

def save_csv():
    """Write all updated labels to CSV_OUTPUT."""
    with open(CSV_OUTPUT, 'w', newline='', encoding='utf-8') as f:
        writer = csv.writer(f)
        writer.writerow(["crop_filename", "recognized_text"])
        for s in samples:
            writer.writerow([s["filename"], s["label"]])
    return f"Labels saved to {CSV_OUTPUT}!"

# Gradio startup function -> load 1st sample
def on_start():
    return load_sample(0)  # (image, label, idx=0)

# --- BUILD THE INTERFACE ---
with gr.Blocks() as demo:
    gr.Markdown("""
    # Handwriting Labeling
    Type your correction and press **Enter**:
    - That **saves** the label
    - Moves to the **next** image
    - **Auto-focuses** the text box so you can keep typing quickly.
    """)

    state_idx = gr.State(value=0)            # track which sample we are on
    image_out = gr.Image(type="pil")         # display the cropped image
    label_box = gr.Textbox(label="Corrected Label")
    status = gr.Label()

    # On app load, show the first sample
    demo.load(
        fn=on_start,
        inputs=None,
        outputs=[image_out, label_box, state_idx]
    )

    # Pressing Enter in label_box calls handle_submit
    # `_js` snippet auto-focuses and selects the text box after the Python callback
    label_box.submit(
        fn=handle_submit,
        inputs=[label_box, state_idx],
        outputs=[image_out, label_box, state_idx],
        _js="""
          (label, idx) => {
            // Wait a moment for Gradio to update the component
            setTimeout(() => {
              const textbox = document.querySelector('textarea[id^="component-"]');
              if (textbox) {
                textbox.focus();
                textbox.select();
              }
            }, 100);
          }
        """
    )

    # Button to save the final CSV
    btn_save = gr.Button("Save CSV")
    btn_save.click(
        fn=save_csv,
        inputs=[],
        outputs=status
    )

demo.launch()

Key Points

    label_box.submit(..., _js="...")
        The _js callback runs in the browser after the Python function handle_submit returns.
        We add a small setTimeout(...) to wait ~100ms for Gradio’s UI update. Then we focus & select the <textarea>.
    No Overwrites: We read from CSV_INPUT but write corrected labels to CSV_OUTPUT, preserving your original input.
    Press Enter → next image. The text box is automatically selected, so you can instantly start typing again.

2. Usage Flow

    Run the script (either on your local machine with python script.py or in a Colab/Enterprise notebook cell).
    A Gradio interface appears at a local or public link.
    The first word image is shown, the recognized text is in the text box.
    Type the correct label (or do partial edits) and press Enter:
        The label is saved in memory, index increments, new image loads, text box is auto-focused.
    Repeat for all images.
    When done (or periodically), click “Save CSV” to write out a new recognized_words_labeled.csv.

3. Troubleshooting

    No Focus? If your environment has script blockers or certain corporate policies, the inline JS might be prevented from running. You can check your browser console for errors.
    Large Datasets: For 1000s of images, consider chunking or a bigger memory approach.
    Previous Button: If you need to go back occasionally, add a button that calls a function go_prev(current_label, idx) to set idx - 1. Then you’d also pass a _js snippet to focus again.

With this approach, you can label/correct text very quickly—Press Enter, type, Enter, type, etc.—and automatically preserve the original file while saving your updated labels in a separate CSV.
You said:
this gives me an error: TypeError: EventListener._setup.<locals>.event_trigger() got an unexpected keyword argument '_js'
Reasoned for 8 seconds

That error means your Gradio version does not support the \_js parameter in event handlers. The \_js argument was introduced in Gradio 3.19 (roughly), so older versions throw an error. Here are two ways to fix it:

    Upgrade your Gradio version so \_js is recognized.
    Fallback to a small “HTML injection” technique, where we return a snippet of JavaScript in a separate output component.

Below, I’ll show you both methods.
1) Method A: Upgrade Gradio

    Check your Gradio version:

pip show gradio

If it’s below 3.19, upgrade:

    pip install --upgrade gradio

    Re-run your code. If your environment now recognizes the \_js parameter, the original snippet will work.

However, if you cannot upgrade due to admin or environment restrictions, see Method B below.
2) Method B: Fallback to “HTML Injection”

Here’s a working pattern without using \_js. We’ll return a JavaScript snippet as an HTML component each time the user submits. That snippet will focus and select the textbox, mimicking the _js behavior.
Revised Example Code

import gradio as gr
import csv
import os
from PIL import Image

CSV_INPUT = "recognized_words.csv"
CSV_OUTPUT = "recognized_words_labeled.csv"
IMAGE_FOLDER = "word_crops"

samples = []
with open(CSV_INPUT, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as f:
    reader = csv.DictReader(f)
    for row in reader:
        samples.append({
            "filename": row["crop_filename"],
            "label": row["recognized_text"]
        })

def load_sample(idx):
    idx = max(0, min(len(samples) - 1, idx))
    sample = samples[idx]
    img_path = os.path.join(IMAGE_FOLDER, os.path.basename(sample["filename"]))
    if os.path.exists(img_path):
        pil_img = Image.open(img_path)
    else:
        pil_img = Image.new("RGB", (300,100), color="red")
    return pil_img, sample["label"], idx

def handle_submit(current_label, idx):
    # 1) Save label
    samples[idx]["label"] = current_label

    # 2) Move to next
    new_idx = min(len(samples)-1, idx+1)
    pil_img, lbl, new_idx = load_sample(new_idx)

    # 3) Return a small JS snippet that auto-focuses the next text box
    focus_script = """
    <script>
      setTimeout(() => {
        const tb = document.querySelector('textarea[id^="component-"]');
        if (tb) {
          tb.focus();
          tb.select();
        }
      }, 100);
    </script>
    """
    return pil_img, lbl, new_idx, focus_script

def save_csv():
    with open(CSV_OUTPUT, 'w', newline='', encoding='utf-8') as f:
        writer = csv.writer(f)
        writer.writerow(["crop_filename", "recognized_text"])
        for s in samples:
            writer.writerow([s["filename"], s["label"]])
    return f"Labels saved to {CSV_OUTPUT}!"

def on_start():
    return load_sample(0)  # image, label, idx=0

with gr.Blocks() as demo:
    gr.Markdown("""
    # Handwriting Labeling
    Type/correct the text, press **Enter** to move to the next image, 
    and the text box should re-focus automatically.
    """)

    state_idx = gr.State(value=0)
    image_out = gr.Image(type="pil")
    label_box = gr.Textbox(label="Corrected Label")
    focus_html = gr.HTML("")  # We'll display JS snippet here
    status = gr.Label()

    # Initial load -> first sample
    demo.load(fn=on_start, inputs=None, outputs=[image_out, label_box, state_idx])

    # Press Enter -> handle_submit, plus we output the focus_html for auto-focus
    label_box.submit(
        fn=handle_submit,
        inputs=[label_box, state_idx],
        outputs=[image_out, label_box, state_idx, focus_html]
    )

    # Save CSV
    btn_save = gr.Button("Save CSV")
    btn_save.click(fn=save_csv, inputs=[], outputs=status)

demo.launch()

What Changed?

    We added a focus_html = gr.HTML("") component to hold the JavaScript snippet.
    In handle_submit, we return (image, label, new_idx, focus_script) → the focus_script is assigned to focus_html.
    This snippet is executed by the browser each time, focusing and selecting the text box after the new sample loads.
    No _js argument is needed on the submit(...) call.

Usage

    Type or correct the text in label_box.
    Press Enter → it updates the label, shows the next image, and automatically focuses/selects the text field for the new label.
    Click Save CSV occasionally or when finished to write updated labels to recognized_words_labeled.csv.

3) Summary

    If you can upgrade Gradio to >=3.19, your original _js approach should work.
    If you’re stuck on an older Gradio, inject a short JS snippet via an HTML output to auto-focus the text field after each submission.

This fallback approach achieves the same result: pressing Enter updates the label, moves to the next image, and auto-highlights the text box so you can type immediately. Enjoy streamlined labeling!

Below is a complete Gradio interface that displays 10 word crops at a time (a “page”) in a scrollable view, each with an editable text box. You can:

    Tab between text fields (each field is auto-selected when you focus it).
    Automatically save each edit in memory when you change the text.
    Navigate to the next/previous page of 10 images.
    Write your final labels to a new CSV file at any time (preserving the original CSV).

This setup works even if your Gradio version doesn’t support the \_js parameter on events. We handle auto-select with a small embedded <script> snippet, and “auto-saving” by listening to each text box’s .change event.

    Note: This is a somewhat advanced usage of Gradio. The code below should work in a recent Gradio 3.x or 4.x environment.

1. Requirements

    A CSV (e.g. recognized_words.csv) with columns:

crop_filename,recognized_text
word_0001.png,HELLO
word_0002.png,WROL
...

A folder word_crops/ containing your images (word_0001.png, etc.).
Gradio installed, e.g.:

    pip install gradio

2. The Full Code

Create a file (e.g. bulk_label_app.py) or paste into a Colab cell:

import gradio as gr
import csv
import os
from PIL import Image

# ========= CONFIG =========
CSV_INPUT = "recognized_words.csv"          # Original CSV (read only)
CSV_OUTPUT = "recognized_words_labeled.csv" # We'll write corrections here
IMAGE_FOLDER = "word_crops"                 # Folder of cropped images

CHUNK_SIZE = 10  # Number of items per "page"

# ========= LOAD DATASET =========
samples = []  # each: {"filename": str, "label": str}
with open(CSV_INPUT, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as f:
    reader = csv.DictReader(f)
    for row in reader:
        samples.append({
            "filename": row["crop_filename"],
            "label": row["recognized_text"]
        })

# Utility: clamp index
def clamp_idx(i):
    return max(0, min(len(samples) - 1, i))

# We'll build a static list of 10 Image components and 10 Textbox components
# for the "page" approach.
images = []
textboxes = []

def make_image_component():
    # We won't do anything special here, just an image placeholder
    return gr.Image(type="pil", interactive=False, label="", show_label=False)

def make_textbox_component():
    # We'll attach .change events to update memory, and rely on JavaScript to auto-select on focus
    return gr.Textbox(label="", show_label=False, lines=1)

for _ in range(CHUNK_SIZE):
    images.append(make_image_component())
    textboxes.append(make_textbox_component())

# ========= STATE & CALLBACKS =========
# We'll keep "chunk_index" in gr.State: which 10-item "page" are we on?
# We'll update the UI each time user goes next/prev or loads the app.

def update_page(chunk_idx):
    """
    Load up to 10 samples starting at chunk_idx*CHUNK_SIZE, 
    and update the images/textboxes with their data.
    Returns no direct output, but uses .update() on each component.
    """
    start = chunk_idx * CHUNK_SIZE
    end = start + CHUNK_SIZE

    for i in range(CHUNK_SIZE):
        comp_index = start + i
        if comp_index < len(samples):
            # Load image
            img_path = os.path.join(IMAGE_FOLDER, os.path.basename(samples[comp_index]["filename"]))
            if os.path.exists(img_path):
                pil_img = Image.open(img_path)
            else:
                pil_img = Image.new("RGB", (300, 100), color="red")

            # Update image & text
            images[i].update(value=pil_img, label=f"Item #{comp_index+1}")
            textboxes[i].update(value=samples[comp_index]["label"], label=f"Text #{comp_index+1}")
        else:
            # If no more data, clear these slots
            images[i].update(value=None, label="")
            textboxes[i].update(value="", label="")

def on_text_change(new_text, i_in_chunk, chunk_idx):
    """
    Called when a text box changes. 
    i_in_chunk is 0..9, chunk_idx is which chunk we're on. 
    We compute the global index in 'samples' and update the label.
    """
    global_idx = chunk_idx*CHUNK_SIZE + i_in_chunk
    if 0 <= global_idx < len(samples):
        samples[global_idx]["label"] = new_text
    # No direct UI update needed, we just store in memory

def navigate(direction, chunk_idx):
    """
    direction: +1 or -1 to move next/prev
    chunk_idx: current chunk index
    """
    new_chunk = chunk_idx + direction
    max_chunk = (len(samples) - 1) // CHUNK_SIZE  # last chunk index
    new_chunk = max(0, min(max_chunk, new_chunk))
    update_page(new_chunk)
    return new_chunk

def save_csv():
    """
    Writes current 'samples' to CSV_OUTPUT
    """
    with open(CSV_OUTPUT, 'w', newline='', encoding='utf-8') as f:
        writer = csv.writer(f)
        writer.writerow(["crop_filename", "recognized_text"])
        for s in samples:
            writer.writerow([s["filename"], s["label"]])
    return f"Labels saved to {CSV_OUTPUT}."

def on_app_start():
    """
    Called when the interface first loads: chunk_idx=0, update_page(0).
    Returns 0 as the initial chunk index.
    """
    update_page(0)
    return 0


# ========== BUILD THE GRADIO INTERFACE ==========
with gr.Blocks() as demo:
    gr.Markdown("""
    # Bulk Labeling: 10 Words at a Time
    
    **Usage**:
    - Each row below has an **image** and a **text box** with the recognized text.
    - You can **tab** between text boxes. Each box auto-selects its text when focused, so you can just type to replace or fix it.
    - Changes are **auto-saved** to memory whenever you edit a box (no button needed for that).
    - Click **Save CSV** anytime to write your current progress to a new file (`recognized_words_labeled.csv`) and preserve your original.
    - Use **Previous** / **Next** to move between 'pages' of 10 samples.
    """)

    # We store the chunk index as a state variable
    chunk_idx_state = gr.State(value=0)

    # We'll embed a small HTML snippet that auto-selects text on focus
    # for each <textarea>.
    focus_script = """
    <script>
    document.addEventListener('DOMContentLoaded', () => {
      document.querySelectorAll('textarea').forEach(el => {
        el.addEventListener('focus', () => {
          el.select();
        });
      });
    });
    </script>
    """
    gr.HTML(focus_script)

    # Display 10 items in rows
    for i in range(CHUNK_SIZE):
        with gr.Row():
            images[i]  # Just place the image
            # For each text box, attach a .change event:
            textboxes[i].change(
                fn=on_text_change,
                inputs=[textboxes[i], gr.Variable(i), chunk_idx_state],
                outputs=[]
            )

    with gr.Row():
        btn_prev = gr.Button("Previous Page")
        btn_next = gr.Button("Next Page")
        btn_save = gr.Button("Save CSV")
    
    status = gr.Label()

    # Hook up navigation
    btn_prev.click(fn=lambda ch: navigate(-1, ch), 
                   inputs=[chunk_idx_state],
                   outputs=[chunk_idx_state])
    btn_next.click(fn=lambda ch: navigate(+1, ch), 
                   inputs=[chunk_idx_state],
                   outputs=[chunk_idx_state])
    
    # Save CSV
    btn_save.click(fn=save_csv, 
                   inputs=[], 
                   outputs=status)

    # On app load
    demo.load(fn=on_app_start, 
              inputs=None, 
              outputs=[chunk_idx_state])

# Launch the interface
demo.launch()

Explanation of Key Parts

    CHUNK_SIZE = 10: We’re showing 10 images/labels per “page.”
    We define images and textboxes lists, each containing 10 Gradio components.
    update_page(chunk_idx): Fills those 10 components with the correct (image, text) from the dataset.
    on_text_change(new_text, i_in_chunk, chunk_idx): Whenever a user changes text in row i_in_chunk (0–9), we compute the global index (chunk_idx*CHUNK_SIZE + i_in_chunk) and update samples in memory. No explicit button needed—this is your “auto-save.”
    navigate(direction, chunk_idx): Moves to the next or previous chunk, then calls update_page(...). Returns the updated chunk index so the UI knows which page we’re on.
    save_csv(): Writes all memory changes to a new file (recognized_words_labeled.csv), preserving the original recognized_words.csv.
    Auto-Select on Focus: We add a <script> that attaches a focus event to each <textarea> after the page loads, so the text is selected when you click or tab into it.

3. Usage Flow

    Run this script locally (python bulk_label_app.py) or in a Colab cell (it will provide a local/public link).
    You’ll see the first 10 items: each row has the image and a text box.
    Tab between text boxes to fix them quickly—each time you leave a box, the .change event updates your in-memory labels.
    Click Next Page to see the next chunk of 10, etc.
    Save CSV any time you want to persist changes to recognized_words_labeled.csv.

This “bulk labeling” approach is more efficient than one-at-a-time for large data, especially when your recognized text is mostly correct and you only need small edits. Enjoy your streamlined labeling workflow!

Below is a modified “bulk labeling” approach that writes corrections to disk immediately whenever you change a text field (instead of storing them in memory until you press “Save CSV”). Each time you edit a text box, it:

    Updates the label in memory.
    Immediately writes the entire dataset to the new CSV file on disk.

    Caution: For very large datasets or rapid typing, this can slow things down or thrash your disk. Consider whether you truly need immediate saves, or if a “periodic save” button is sufficient. But if your dataset is moderate and you prefer guaranteed persistence, here’s how.

1. Full Example Code

import gradio as gr
import csv
import os
from PIL import Image

# ========= CONFIG =========
CSV_INPUT = "recognized_words.csv"           # Original CSV (read only)
CSV_OUTPUT = "recognized_words_labeled.csv"  # We'll write updated labels here
IMAGE_FOLDER = "word_crops"
CHUNK_SIZE = 10  # Number of items (words) per "page"

# ========= LOAD DATASET =========
samples = []  # each element: {"filename": str, "label": str}
with open(CSV_INPUT, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as f:
    reader = csv.DictReader(f)
    for row in reader:
        samples.append({
            "filename": row["crop_filename"],
            "label": row["recognized_text"]
        })

def clamp_idx(i):
    return max(0, min(len(samples) - 1, i))

# We'll create CHUNK_SIZE image components and textboxes
images = []
textboxes = []

for _ in range(CHUNK_SIZE):
    images.append(gr.Image(type="pil", interactive=False, show_label=False))
    textboxes.append(gr.Textbox(show_label=False, lines=1))

def update_page(chunk_idx):
    """
    Fill the 10 components with the correct data for chunk_idx.
    chunk_idx: which 'page' we're on (0,1,2,...).
    """
    start = chunk_idx * CHUNK_SIZE
    end = start + CHUNK_SIZE

    for i in range(CHUNK_SIZE):
        comp_index = start + i
        if comp_index < len(samples):
            # Load the image
            img_path = os.path.join(IMAGE_FOLDER, os.path.basename(samples[comp_index]["filename"]))
            if os.path.exists(img_path):
                pil_img = Image.open(img_path)
            else:
                pil_img = Image.new("RGB", (300,100), color="red")
            
            images[i].update(value=pil_img, label=f"Item {comp_index+1}")
            textboxes[i].update(value=samples[comp_index]["label"], label=f"Label {comp_index+1}")
        else:
            # No more data => clear
            images[i].update(value=None, label="")
            textboxes[i].update(value="", label="")

def save_csv():
    """
    Writes the entire 'samples' list to CSV_OUTPUT.
    Called after every text change in this version.
    """
    with open(CSV_OUTPUT, 'w', newline='', encoding='utf-8') as f:
        writer = csv.writer(f)
        writer.writerow(["crop_filename", "recognized_text"])
        for s in samples:
            writer.writerow([s["filename"], s["label"]])

def on_text_change(new_text, i_in_chunk, chunk_idx):
    """
    Called whenever a text box changes.
    1) Calculate global index
    2) Update in-memory label
    3) Immediately save CSV
    No direct UI output, but ensures data is safe on disk.
    """
    global_idx = chunk_idx*CHUNK_SIZE + i_in_chunk
    if 0 <= global_idx < len(samples):
        samples[global_idx]["label"] = new_text
        save_csv()  # Immediately write to disk

def navigate(direction, chunk_idx):
    """
    Move chunk_idx forward or backward by 'direction'.
    Update the page and return new chunk index.
    """
    new_chunk = chunk_idx + direction
    max_chunk = (len(samples) - 1) // CHUNK_SIZE
    new_chunk = max(0, min(max_chunk, new_chunk))
    update_page(new_chunk)
    return new_chunk

def on_app_start():
    """
    Called at Gradio launch. We start on chunk_idx=0 and load that page.
    """
    update_page(0)
    return 0  # chunk_idx=0 as initial state

# ========== BUILD THE GRADIO INTERFACE ==========
with gr.Blocks() as demo:
    gr.Markdown("""
    # Bulk Labeling with Immediate Disk Saves
    
    - Each page shows up to 10 word crops.
    - Edit the text, and as soon as you change it, the CSV is **written to disk**.
      This ensures no data loss, but may slow large datasets.
    - Use **Previous** / **Next** to move through pages of 10.
    """)

    chunk_idx_state = gr.State(value=0)

    # A small script that auto-selects text in a <textarea> on focus
    focus_script = """
    <script>
    document.addEventListener('DOMContentLoaded', () => {
      document.querySelectorAll('textarea').forEach(el => {
        el.addEventListener('focus', () => {
          el.select();
        });
      });
    });
    </script>
    """
    gr.HTML(focus_script)

    # Layout: 10 rows, each with (Image, Textbox)
    for i in range(CHUNK_SIZE):
        with gr.Row():
            images[i]  # no callback
            textboxes[i].change(
                fn=on_text_change,
                inputs=[textboxes[i], gr.Variable(i), chunk_idx_state],
                outputs=[]
            )

    # Nav buttons
    with gr.Row():
        btn_prev = gr.Button("Previous Page")
        btn_next = gr.Button("Next Page")

    # Hook up navigation
    btn_prev.click(fn=lambda ch: navigate(-1, ch), inputs=[chunk_idx_state], outputs=[chunk_idx_state])
    btn_next.click(fn=lambda ch: navigate(+1, ch), inputs=[chunk_idx_state], outputs=[chunk_idx_state])

    # On load
    demo.load(fn=on_app_start, inputs=None, outputs=[chunk_idx_state])

demo.launch()

Key Changes vs. Previous Example

    Immediate Disk Writes: In on_text_change, after updating samples[global_idx]["label"], we call save_csv(). This ensures the CSV is always up to date.
    We removed the “Save CSV” button—not strictly necessary anymore since changes are saved automatically. If you want to keep a manual button, you can add it back.
    Auto-Select on focus: a small <script> sets up a focus event on all <textarea> elements so the text is highlighted when you tab or click into it.

2. Usage Flow

    Run this script.
    You’ll see up to 10 word crops in rows. For each row:
        The left panel is the image.
        The right panel is the text box with the recognized text.
    Edit a text box. As soon as you change it and press Enter/tab away, on_text_change is triggered—writes the entire dataset to recognized_words_labeled.csv.
    Navigate pages with “Previous Page” or “Next Page.”

Your progress is always persisted on disk.
3. Performance Considerations

    Frequent Writes: If you have thousands of images and do a lot of quick edits, rewriting the entire CSV for each keystroke could be slow. You may want to only auto-save once every few seconds or only after “blur” if you’re comfortable with a small risk of losing partial edits.
    Partial Data: Because we overwrite recognized_words_labeled.csv each time, the file always reflects the latest state. If you want versioning, you might rename or timestamp each write.

Final Thoughts

With this approach, you no longer rely on an in-memory stash—each text change triggers a write to disk. This ensures minimal risk of data loss, with the tradeoff of extra disk I/O. If you find it too slow, you can revert to a “Save CSV” button or batch updates. Otherwise, enjoy the convenience of continuous auto-saving!
You said:
Going back to the previous code with in-memory save, I get this error: 
AttributeError: module 'gradio' has no attribute 'Variable'
Reasoned for a few seconds

Cause of the Error
Gradio no longer provides a gr.Variable component. That was once mentioned in older examples or internal docs, but it doesn’t exist in current releases. This causes the error:

AttributeError: module 'gradio' has no attribute 'Variable'

Context
You were using a snippet like:

textboxes[i].change(
    fn=on_text_change,
    inputs=[textboxes[i], gr.Variable(i), chunk_idx_state],
    outputs=[]
)

where gr.Variable(i) was intended to pass a static integer i into the callback. That no longer works in the current Gradio API.
1. Workarounds to Pass a Static Value

We can’t directly use gr.Variable(i), so we need another way to provide “which row index” or other static info to the function. Below are two common patterns:
Pattern A: Closure / Factory Function

We create a small factory function that “bakes in” the index i_in_chunk at definition time. For example:

def build_change_callback(i_in_chunk):
    def wrapper(new_text, chunk_idx):
        on_text_change(new_text, i_in_chunk, chunk_idx)
    return wrapper

    Here, i_in_chunk is captured in the function closure.
    Then, each textbox calls that custom function, passing only the dynamic inputs that do exist (the user’s typed text, and chunk_idx from gr.State).

Full Example (snippet):

# Instead of gr.Variable(i), do this:
for i in range(CHUNK_SIZE):
    def build_change_callback(i_in_chunk):
        def wrapper(new_text, chunk_idx):
            # call your real function with i_in_chunk
            on_text_change(new_text, i_in_chunk, chunk_idx)
        return wrapper

    textboxes[i].change(
        fn=build_change_callback(i),
        inputs=[textboxes[i], chunk_idx_state],  # only the dynamic inputs
        outputs=[]
    )

This way:

    Each textboxes[i] has its own closure that knows i_in_chunk = i.
    When the user changes text in that row, we call wrapper(new_text, chunk_idx) → which calls on_text_change(new_text, i_in_chunk, chunk_idx).

Pattern B: Partial Function

Similar to a closure, but using functools.partial:

import functools

for i in range(CHUNK_SIZE):
    partial_fn = functools.partial(on_text_change, i_in_chunk=i)
    textboxes[i].change(
        fn=partial_fn,
        inputs=[textboxes[i], chunk_idx_state],
        outputs=[]
    )

    Here, partial_fn(new_text, chunk_idx) is effectively on_text_change(new_text, chunk_idx, i_in_chunk=i).
    We just need to adjust the signature of on_text_change to accept the arguments in the right order or with default keywords.

For example:

def on_text_change(new_text, chunk_idx, i_in_chunk=0):
    # i_in_chunk is bound via partial
    global_idx = chunk_idx*CHUNK_SIZE + i_in_chunk
    ...

2. Putting It All Together

Below is a simplified snippet reminiscent of your “in-memory save” code that displays 10 word crops, each with a text box. We use Pattern A: Closure so each row knows its own index.

import gradio as gr
import csv
import os
from PIL import Image

CSV_INPUT = "recognized_words.csv"
IMAGE_FOLDER = "word_crops"
CHUNK_SIZE = 10

samples = []
# load your CSV
...

def update_page(chunk_idx):
    # fill 10 components
    ...

def on_text_change(new_text, i_in_chunk, chunk_idx):
    # i_in_chunk is the row index, 0..9
    # chunk_idx is the page index
    global_idx = chunk_idx * CHUNK_SIZE + i_in_chunk
    # update in-memory label
    samples[global_idx]["label"] = new_text
    # no immediate CSV write unless you want that

def navigate(direction, chunk_idx):
    # next/previous page
    ...

def on_app_start():
    # load chunk_idx=0
    update_page(0)
    return 0

images = [gr.Image(type="pil", interactive=False) for _ in range(CHUNK_SIZE)]
textboxes = [gr.Textbox(lines=1) for _ in range(CHUNK_SIZE)]

with gr.Blocks() as demo:
    chunk_idx_state = gr.State(value=0)

    def build_change_callback(i_in_chunk):
        def wrapper(new_text, chunk_idx):
            on_text_change(new_text, i_in_chunk, chunk_idx)
        return wrapper

    for i in range(CHUNK_SIZE):
        with gr.Row():
            images[i]
            textboxes[i].change(
                fn=build_change_callback(i),
                inputs=[textboxes[i], chunk_idx_state],
                outputs=[]
            )

    btn_prev = gr.Button("Previous Page")
    btn_next = gr.Button("Next Page")

    btn_prev.click(fn=lambda ch: navigate(-1, ch),
                   inputs=[chunk_idx_state],
                   outputs=[chunk_idx_state])
    btn_next.click(fn=lambda ch: navigate(1, ch),
                   inputs=[chunk_idx_state],
                   outputs=[chunk_idx_state])

    # on load
    demo.load(fn=on_app_start, inputs=None, outputs=[chunk_idx_state])

demo.launch()

Key: Notice how textboxes[i].change(...) calls build_change_callback(i) to embed the row index in a closure. There’s no gr.Variable(i) needed.
3. Summary

    gr.Variable is not part of the current Gradio API, hence the error.
    To pass a static integer (like the row index i) to your callback, use a closure or partial.
    The rest of your in-memory page-based labeling workflow remains the same.

With that fix, you can preserve the same functionality—multiple text boxes, each referencing a unique index—without using a non-existent gr.Variable.

Below is a complete, self-contained Gradio example that shows 10 word crops per “page,” each with a textbox for editing. It uses the closure trick to pass a row index without gr.Variable. If you provide a valid CSV (recognized_words.csv) and a folder of cropped images (word_crops/), you should see the interface with 10 rows of (image, text field) at a time, with “Previous Page” and “Next Page” buttons for navigation.
1. How to Use

    Prepare your data:
        A CSV called recognized_words.csv with columns:

        crop_filename,recognized_text
        word_0001.png,HELLO
        word_0002.png,WROL
        ...

        A folder word_crops/ containing images named exactly as in the CSV’s crop_filename column.

    Put this Python script (e.g. bulk_labeling.py) in the same directory.

    Run python bulk_labeling.py (locally) or paste into a Colab cell. You’ll get a Gradio link.

    Open the link; you should see 10 images + 10 textboxes. Modify text as needed. (This snippet just saves the new text in memory, not to disk—see the next notes for saving.)

2. Full Example Code

import gradio as gr
import csv
import os
from PIL import Image

# =====================
# CONFIG
# =====================
CSV_INPUT = "recognized_words.csv"   # CSV with crop_filename,recognized_text
IMAGE_FOLDER = "word_crops"          # Folder containing the images
CHUNK_SIZE = 10                      # 10 items per "page"

# =====================
# LOAD SAMPLES
# =====================
samples = []  # each: {"filename": str, "label": str}
if os.path.exists(CSV_INPUT):
    with open(CSV_INPUT, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as f:
        reader = csv.DictReader(f)
        for row in reader:
            samples.append({
                "filename": row["crop_filename"],
                "label": row["recognized_text"]
            })
else:
    # If you don't have a CSV, create dummy data for demonstration
    print(f"Warning: {CSV_INPUT} not found, creating dummy data.")
    for i in range(25):
        samples.append({
            "filename": f"word_{i}.png",
            "label": f"Text{i}"
        })

# =====================
# BUILD COMPONENT LISTS
# =====================
# We'll display 10 images + 10 textboxes per page
images = [gr.Image(type="pil", interactive=False) for _ in range(CHUNK_SIZE)]
textboxes = [gr.Textbox(lines=1) for _ in range(CHUNK_SIZE)]

# =====================
# HELPER FUNCTIONS
# =====================
def clamp_idx(i):
    return max(0, min(len(samples) - 1, i))

def update_page(chunk_idx):
    """
    Fills the 10 image/textbox pairs with data from samples, 
    starting at chunk_idx * CHUNK_SIZE.
    """
    start = chunk_idx * CHUNK_SIZE
    end = start + CHUNK_SIZE

    for i in range(CHUNK_SIZE):
        comp_index = start + i
        if comp_index < len(samples):
            # Load the image from disk
            img_file = os.path.join(IMAGE_FOLDER, samples[comp_index]["filename"])
            if os.path.exists(img_file):
                pil_img = Image.open(img_file)
            else:
                # Placeholder if missing
                pil_img = Image.new("RGB", (300, 100), color="red")

            # Update the components
            images[i].update(value=pil_img, label=f"Image #{comp_index+1}")
            textboxes[i].update(value=samples[comp_index]["label"],
                                label=f"Label #{comp_index+1}")
        else:
            # Clear if no data
            images[i].update(value=None, label="")
            textboxes[i].update(value="", label="")

def on_text_change(new_text, row_in_chunk, chunk_idx):
    """
    Called whenever a textbox changes.
    row_in_chunk: which of the 10 textboxes triggered this
    chunk_idx: which page we're on
    """
    global_idx = chunk_idx * CHUNK_SIZE + row_in_chunk
    if 0 <= global_idx < len(samples):
        samples[global_idx]["label"] = new_text
        print(f"Updated sample[{global_idx}] to: {new_text}")
    # (In this snippet, we do NOT automatically save to disk—just memory.)

def navigate(direction, chunk_idx):
    """
    Moves chunk_idx by +/-1 for next/previous page,
    updates the page, returns new chunk index.
    """
    max_chunk = (len(samples) - 1) // CHUNK_SIZE
    new_idx = chunk_idx + direction
    new_idx = max(0, min(max_chunk, new_idx))
    update_page(new_idx)
    return new_idx

def on_app_start():
    # Start on page 0
    update_page(0)
    return 0

# =====================
# BUILD THE GRADIO APP
# =====================
with gr.Blocks() as demo:
    gr.Markdown("""
    # Bulk Labeling (In-Memory)
    Displays up to 10 items per page. 
    Edit text in the boxes, changes are stored in memory (see console logs).
    Use "Previous" or "Next" to navigate pages.
    """)

    # We'll keep track of which chunk we're on in a Gradio State
    chunk_idx_state = gr.State(value=0)

    # A script that auto-selects text on focus, for convenience
    focus_script = """
    <script>
    document.addEventListener('DOMContentLoaded', () => {
      document.querySelectorAll('textarea').forEach(el => {
        el.addEventListener('focus', () => {
          el.select();
        });
      });
    });
    </script>
    """
    gr.HTML(focus_script)

    # Make a vertical layout of 10 rows
    for i in range(CHUNK_SIZE):
        # We'll create a closure so each text box "knows" its row_in_chunk
        def make_callback(i_in_chunk):
            def wrapper(new_text, cur_chunk_idx):
                on_text_change(new_text, i_in_chunk, cur_chunk_idx)
            return wrapper

        with gr.Row():
            images[i]
            # Each text box calls a custom callback with (text, chunk_idx_state)
            textboxes[i].change(
                fn=make_callback(i),
                inputs=[textboxes[i], chunk_idx_state],
                outputs=[]
            )

    with gr.Row():
        btn_prev = gr.Button("Previous Page")
        btn_next = gr.Button("Next Page")

    # Hook up navigation
    btn_prev.click(fn=lambda ch: navigate(-1, ch),
                   inputs=[chunk_idx_state],
                   outputs=[chunk_idx_state])
    btn_next.click(fn=lambda ch: navigate(+1, ch),
                   inputs=[chunk_idx_state],
                   outputs=[chunk_idx_state])

    # On load, we set chunk_idx=0 and show that page
    demo.load(fn=on_app_start, 
              inputs=None, 
              outputs=[chunk_idx_state])

demo.launch()

3. Notes & Next Steps

    Why No Save to Disk?
        This snippet only updates samples in memory. If you need to save the updated labels to a new CSV, add a “Save CSV” button (or auto-save on each change).
        For example, you can define def save_csv(): to write samples to recognized_words_labeled.csv. Then call it in a new button or in on_text_change.

    Auto-Focus
        The snippet includes a small <script> that selects text in a textbox on focus. This helps you quickly type corrections.

    Missing Images
        If an image is missing from word_crops/, we display a red placeholder.
        Check your console logs if you see an “Updated sample[4] to ...” message, indicating the memory is updating.

    Large Datasets
        If you have thousands of lines, paging 10 at a time is a good approach. If you prefer 20 or 50, change CHUNK_SIZE.

    In Colab
        If you’re in Colab (including Colab Enterprise), you’ll see an inline or external link to the Gradio app. If you see policy blocks or need a tunnel, see prior instructions.

That’s it!

When you run this code with a valid CSV and images, you should see 10 rows of (image, text field) for your recognized words. Edits go into memory, and you can navigate pages. This is a fully functional starting point for a multi-word labeling interface in Gradio without gr.Variable.

Below is a revised Gradio snippet that should visibly display both the images and the text fields. The key is ensuring each textbox is explicitly placed in the layout so the user can see it. In Gradio, simply referencing textboxes[i].change(...) is not enough—you also must show the component in the interface (for example, by placing textboxes[i] in a Row or Column).

This example:

    Loads a CSV of up to N (crop_filename, recognized_text) entries into an in-memory list samples.
    Displays 10 items per “page” in the interface: each item has an image on the left and a textbox on the right.
    Stores the user’s typed text in memory whenever you change a textbox (but does not immediately write to a CSV).
    Lets you navigate pages with “Previous Page” and “Next Page” buttons.

1. Create or Use a CSV + Images

    A CSV named recognized_words.csv with columns:

    crop_filename,recognized_text
    word_0001.png,HELLO
    word_0002.png,SOMEWORD
    ...

    A folder word_crops/ containing images named exactly like word_0001.png, etc.

2. The Complete Code

import gradio as gr
import csv
import os
from PIL import Image

# ===================================================
# CONFIG
# ===================================================
CSV_INPUT = "recognized_words.csv"   # Source CSV
IMAGE_FOLDER = "word_crops"          # Folder with images
CHUNK_SIZE = 10                      # 10 items per page

# ===================================================
# LOAD SAMPLES
# ===================================================
samples = []  # each: {"filename":..., "label":...}

if os.path.exists(CSV_INPUT):
    with open(CSV_INPUT, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as f:
        reader = csv.DictReader(f)
        for row in reader:
            samples.append({
                "filename": row["crop_filename"],
                "label": row["recognized_text"]
            })
else:
    # No CSV found -> create some dummy data for demonstration
    print(f"Warning: {CSV_INPUT} not found, creating 25 dummy samples.")
    for i in range(25):
        samples.append({
            "filename": f"word_{i}.png",
            "label": f"Text{i}"
        })

# ===================================================
# BUILD COMPONENT LISTS
# ===================================================
# We'll have 10 'rows' each with an Image + Textbox
images = [gr.Image(type="pil", interactive=False) for _ in range(CHUNK_SIZE)]
textboxes = [gr.Textbox(lines=1) for _ in range(CHUNK_SIZE)]

# ===================================================
# HELPER FUNCTIONS
# ===================================================

def update_page(chunk_idx: int):
    """
    Fills the 10 image/textbox pairs with data from `samples`, 
    starting at chunk_idx * CHUNK_SIZE.
    """
    start = chunk_idx * CHUNK_SIZE
    for i in range(CHUNK_SIZE):
        global_index = start + i
        if global_index < len(samples):
            # Load the image
            filename = samples[global_index]["filename"]
            label_text = samples[global_index]["label"]
            img_path = os.path.join(IMAGE_FOLDER, filename)

            if os.path.exists(img_path):
                pil_img = Image.open(img_path)
            else:
                pil_img = Image.new("RGB", (300, 100), color="red")  # placeholder

            images[i].update(value=pil_img, label=f"Image #{global_index+1}")
            textboxes[i].update(value=label_text, label=f"Label #{global_index+1}")
        else:
            # Clear components if no more data
            images[i].update(value=None, label="")
            textboxes[i].update(value="", label="")

def on_text_change(new_text, row_in_chunk, chunk_idx):
    """
    Called whenever a user changes the text in one of the text boxes.
    row_in_chunk: 0..9 (which row in the current page)
    chunk_idx: current page index
    """
    global_index = chunk_idx * CHUNK_SIZE + row_in_chunk
    if 0 <= global_index < len(samples):
        samples[global_index]["label"] = new_text
        print(f"Updated sample[{global_index}] to: {new_text}")

def navigate_page(direction, current_chunk_idx):
    """
    Moves chunk_idx by +/-1, updates the page, returns the new chunk_idx.
    """
    max_chunk = (len(samples) - 1) // CHUNK_SIZE
    new_chunk = current_chunk_idx + direction
    new_chunk = max(0, min(max_chunk, new_chunk))

    update_page(new_chunk)
    return new_chunk

def on_app_start():
    """
    Called when the Gradio app first loads:
    - set chunk_idx=0
    - update the page
    """
    update_page(0)
    return 0  # the initial chunk index

# ===================================================
# BUILD THE GRADIO INTERFACE
# ===================================================
with gr.Blocks() as demo:
    gr.Markdown("""
    # Bulk Labeling (In-Memory)
    - Displays up to 10 items per page.
    - Edit text in each textbox; changes are stored in memory (shown in console logs).
    - Use **Previous Page** / **Next Page** to navigate.
    """)

    # Keep track of which page we're on
    chunk_idx_state = gr.State(value=0)

    # A snippet to auto-select text on focus
    focus_script = """
    <script>
    document.addEventListener('DOMContentLoaded', () => {
      document.querySelectorAll('textarea').forEach(el => {
        el.addEventListener('focus', () => el.select());
      });
    });
    </script>
    """
    gr.HTML(focus_script)

    # For each row, we display (Image, Textbox)
    for i in range(CHUNK_SIZE):
        def build_change_callback(row_in_chunk):
            def wrapper(new_text, cur_chunk_idx):
                on_text_change(new_text, row_in_chunk, cur_chunk_idx)
            return wrapper

        with gr.Row():
            # Show image
            images[i]
            # Show textbox
            textboxes[i].change(
                fn=build_change_callback(i),
                inputs=[textboxes[i], chunk_idx_state],
                outputs=[]
            )

    # Navigation buttons
    with gr.Row():
        btn_prev = gr.Button("Previous Page")
        btn_next = gr.Button("Next Page")

    # Hook them up
    btn_prev.click(fn=lambda ch: navigate_page(-1, ch),
                   inputs=[chunk_idx_state],
                   outputs=[chunk_idx_state])
    btn_next.click(fn=lambda ch: navigate_page(+1, ch),
                   inputs=[chunk_idx_state],
                   outputs=[chunk_idx_state])

    # On app load -> chunk_idx=0
    demo.load(fn=on_app_start, 
              inputs=None, 
              outputs=[chunk_idx_state])

demo.launch()

Explanation of the Critical Lines

    with gr.Row(): images[i] textboxes[i]: By placing images[i] and textboxes[i] in the same block, the user sees both components on the same row.
    textboxes[i].change(fn=..., inputs=[textboxes[i], chunk_idx_state]): This sets up the callback that fires whenever text changes, passing the user’s new text plus the current chunk (page) index.
    build_change_callback(i): We can’t use gr.Variable(i), so we define a function that returns a closure capturing i_in_chunk. This way, each row “knows” its index in the page.
    update_page(new_chunk): Loads the next or previous chunk of 10 items so they appear in the interface.

3. Why It Might “Not Display”

    Missing or Invalid CSV: If your CSV has no data or you’re not referencing correct columns, you might see empty rows. Check the console logs for warnings.
    Empty word_crops/ folder or different filenames: If the PNG files aren’t found, images appear as red placeholders.
    No text: If the CSV has blank recognized_text or if the script used a partial example, text might appear empty—but the text boxes themselves will show up as lines you can type in.

4. Saving Data

Currently, we only store changes in memory. If you want to write them to a new CSV file, you can:

    Define a function like:

def save_csv():
    with open("recognized_words_labeled.csv", 'w', newline='', encoding='utf-8') as f:
        writer = csv.writer(f)
        writer.writerow(["crop_filename", "recognized_text"])
        for s in samples:
            writer.writerow([s["filename"], s["label"]])
    return "Saved!"

Add a “Save CSV” button in the interface:

    btn_save = gr.Button("Save CSV")
    btn_save.click(fn=save_csv, inputs=[], outputs=some_label_for_status)

Now you can preserve the updated text in a new file.
5. Conclusion

    This snippet explicitly places the textbox in the UI, ensuring you see the input field on each row.
    When you run the code, you should see 10 rows of (image, text input).
    Changing any text calls the callback and updates the in-memory dataset.
    This approach should fix the previous issue of text fields not showing up. If you still don’t see them, double-check your environment or look for warnings in the console logs.

If no UI (no images or text fields) appears at all, it’s usually not the code itself—but rather the environment in which you’re running Gradio. Common culprits include:

    Colab (including Enterprise) not displaying inline
    Browser or corporate firewall blocking the local web server
    You must open a public or local link in a new tab to view the UI
    Gradio compatibility or environment issues (older/newer versions)

Below are some steps and a minimal test snippet to confirm if Gradio itself is working in your environment. If that minimal test doesn’t show anything, the problem is almost certainly environment-based, not your OCR labeling code.
1. Confirm Gradio Works With a Minimal Test

Try this tiny snippet in the same environment (Colab Enterprise or local) where your bigger script is failing:

import gradio as gr

def greet(name):
    return f"Hello, {name}!"

demo = gr.Interface(
    fn=greet, 
    inputs="text", 
    outputs="text", 
    title="Minimal Gradio Test",
    description="Type a name and hit enter."
)

demo.launch()

What Should Happen?

    After running, you should see either:
        An inline UI in your Colab cell (with a text box and output area),
        Or a message in the output:

        Running on local URL:  http://127.0.0.1:7860
        Running on public URL: https://xxxx.gradio.app

        Then you can click the link or copy-paste it into your browser to see the interface.

If You Still See Nothing

    Check your console or notebook logs. Possibly your environment is blocking network connections.
    If you’re in Colab Enterprise, some organizations lock down ephemeral ports or iframes. You might need an external tunneling approach or a certain config.
    In normal Colab, sometimes you must expand the cell output or click a link that says “Running on public URL…”
    Try demo.launch(share=True) to force a public link:

    demo.launch(share=True)

    Then open that link in a new browser tab.

If this minimal snippet doesn’t display, no larger snippet will. You’ll need to address the environment issues first.
2. A Tiny Blocks-Based Test (Showing an Image + Textbox)

If you want a Blocks approach (like your OCR labeling code) but simpler:

import gradio as gr

with gr.Blocks() as demo:
    gr.Markdown("## Mini Test: Should display an image & text field below.")

    with gr.Row():
        test_img = gr.Image(value="https://raw.githubusercontent.com/gradio-app/gradio/main/test/test_files/bus.png")
        test_text = gr.Textbox(value="Sample text here")

demo.launch()

Expected Behavior

    A title: “Mini Test: Should display an image & text field below.”
    A single row with an image (the bus from a test URL) and a textbox containing “Sample text here.”

If that still shows nothing:

    Again, check logs for a “Running on local URL… public URL…” message.
    Open the link in a new tab if needed.
    Ensure your org’s Colab Enterprise or firewall policy allows it.

3. Common Gradio Issues in Colab Enterprise

    Inline vs. External: If your environment disallows inline iframes, you might only get a link. You must open that link.
    Firewalls: Some corporate networks block ephemeral ports. If that’s the case, the link might fail to load.
    No Cell Output: Make sure the cell is actually running. In Colab, a busy cell is indicated by the spinning icon. Once it’s complete, you typically see text about the Gradio server.

4. If the Minimal Tests Work, Then Paste in Your OCR Labeling Code

Once you confirm that Gradio is functioning (by seeing at least these test UIs or a link you can open), you can paste the larger “10 words per page” snippet. The interface should show images and text fields if:

    Your CSV is valid (crop_filename,recognized_text columns).
    The images exist (or you see placeholders).
    You open the correct link to view the UI.

5. Summary

    Verify a super-simple Gradio script. If you still see no UI, the issue is environment-related.
    Check if you need to open a link in a new tab or if something in Colab Enterprise is blocking it.
    Once the basic test works, your OCR labeling snippet (with images and text fields) should also appear—provided the code is running, and you’re opening the correct link or viewing the inline frame.

In short: If Gradio is running but you never see its interface, you’re likely dealing with a display/tunneling problem, not an issue in your code that configures textboxes and images. Get the minimal snippet working first, and then your labeling interface should display as expected.
