The minimal tests worked. Let's back up and try an interface presenting one image and text field at a time
Reasoned for 7 seconds

Below is a minimal Gradio interface that presents one image and one text field at a time, letting you navigate forward/back. This approach is simpler than the multi-row layout—ideal if you prefer labeling one item at a time.
What It Does

    Loads a CSV (recognized_words.csv) containing crop_filename,recognized_text.
    Displays a single image (image_out) and a single text field (text_in).
    Updates an in-memory list of (filename, label) when you edit the text and click “Next” or “Previous.”
    (Optional) Has a “Save CSV” button to write out updated labels.

1) Setup Your Data

    CSV: recognized_words.csv

    crop_filename,recognized_text
    word_001.png,HELLO
    word_002.png,FOOBAR
    ...

    Images: A folder word_crops/ with those filenames (e.g. word_001.png).

2) Complete Code Example

import gradio as gr
import csv
import os
from PIL import Image

# =========================================
# CONFIG
# =========================================
CSV_INPUT = "recognized_words.csv"       # Original CSV
CSV_OUTPUT = "recognized_words_labeled.csv"  # We'll optionally save updates here
IMAGE_FOLDER = "word_crops"

# =========================================
# LOAD DATA INTO MEMORY
# =========================================
samples = []  # each: {"filename": str, "label": str}

if os.path.exists(CSV_INPUT):
    with open(CSV_INPUT, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as f:
        reader = csv.DictReader(f)
        for row in reader:
            samples.append({
                "filename": row["crop_filename"],
                "label": row["recognized_text"]
            })
else:
    # If no CSV found, build dummy data
    print(f"Warning: {CSV_INPUT} not found, generating 5 dummy entries.")
    for i in range(5):
        samples.append({
            "filename": f"dummy_{i}.png",
            "label": f"DUMMY{i}"
        })

# A small function to clamp the index within [0, len(samples)-1]
def clamp_index(idx):
    return max(0, min(len(samples) - 1, idx))

# =========================================
# HELPER: GET ITEM
# =========================================
def display_item(idx: int):
    """
    Returns (image, text, idx) for the given sample index,
    or placeholders if out of range.
    """
    idx = clamp_index(idx)
    if idx < len(samples):
        fn = samples[idx]["filename"]
        lbl = samples[idx]["label"]
        img_path = os.path.join(IMAGE_FOLDER, fn)
        if os.path.exists(img_path):
            pil_img = Image.open(img_path)
        else:
            # Red placeholder if missing
            pil_img = Image.new("RGB", (300, 100), color="red")
        return pil_img, lbl, idx
    else:
        return None, "", idx

# =========================================
# CALLBACKS
# =========================================
def go_next(current_text, current_idx):
    """
    1) Save current_text into samples[current_idx].
    2) Move to next index.
    3) Return updated image, text, new_idx.
    """
    current_idx = clamp_index(current_idx)
    # Update memory
    samples[current_idx]["label"] = current_text
    
    # Move forward
    new_idx = current_idx + 1
    if new_idx >= len(samples):
        new_idx = len(samples) - 1  # stay on last
    
    return display_item(new_idx)  # (img, text, new_idx)

def go_prev(current_text, current_idx):
    """
    Similar to go_next but moves backward.
    """
    current_idx = clamp_index(current_idx)
    samples[current_idx]["label"] = current_text
    
    new_idx = current_idx - 1
    if new_idx < 0:
        new_idx = 0
    
    return display_item(new_idx)

def save_csv():
    """
    Writes the in-memory `samples` to a new CSV file (CSV_OUTPUT).
    """
    with open(CSV_OUTPUT, 'w', newline='', encoding='utf-8') as f:
        writer = csv.writer(f)
        writer.writerow(["crop_filename", "recognized_text"])
        for s in samples:
            writer.writerow([s["filename"], s["label"]])
    return f"Saved updated labels to {CSV_OUTPUT}!"

def on_app_start():
    """
    Called once when the app loads:
    Start at index 0.
    """
    return display_item(0)  # (image, text, idx=0)

# =========================================
# BUILD THE GRADIO INTERFACE
# =========================================
with gr.Blocks() as demo:
    gr.Markdown("""
    # Single-Item Labeling
    Shows one image & text at a time. 
    Use **Next** or **Previous** to navigate.
    Changes are stored in memory. 
    Press **Save CSV** to write them out.
    """)

    # We'll keep track of the current index in gr.State
    state_idx = gr.State(value=0)

    # The displayed image
    image_out = gr.Image(type="pil", label="Word Image")
    # The text field
    text_in = gr.Textbox(lines=1, label="Recognized/Corrected Text")

    # Navigation buttons
    with gr.Row():
        btn_prev = gr.Button("Previous")
        btn_next = gr.Button("Next")
    # Save button
    btn_save = gr.Button("Save CSV")
    save_status = gr.Label()

    # WIRE UP THE LOGIC
    # 1) On app load -> show the 0th item
    demo.load(fn=on_app_start, inputs=None, outputs=[image_out, text_in, state_idx])

    # 2) Next
    btn_next.click(
        fn=go_next,
        inputs=[text_in, state_idx],
        outputs=[image_out, text_in, state_idx]
    )

    # 3) Prev
    btn_prev.click(
        fn=go_prev,
        inputs=[text_in, state_idx],
        outputs=[image_out, text_in, state_idx]
    )

    # 4) Save
    btn_save.click(
        fn=save_csv,
        inputs=[],
        outputs=save_status
    )

demo.launch()

How It Works

    Initialization: On app load, on_app_start() returns (image, text, idx=0) for the first sample.
    go_next(...):
        Saves the current text to samples[current_idx]["label"].
        Increments the index to new_idx, clamps if out of range.
        Returns (image, text, new_idx) for the new index to display.
    One At a Time: You only see the current image and text in image_out / text_in.
    Saving: When you click “Save CSV,” save_csv() writes all updated (filename, label) from memory to recognized_words_labeled.csv (or whichever file you choose).

3) Usage

    Run the script (locally or in Colab).
    You’ll see an interface with:
        A single image
        A textbox with the recognized text
        “Previous,” “Next,” and “Save CSV” buttons
    Navigate through your data.
    Edit the text if needed. When you click “Next” or “Previous,” that text is stored in memory.
    Press “Save CSV” when you want to permanently write all changes to disk.

4) Troubleshooting

    No CSV or folder: If recognized_words.csv doesn’t exist, it creates dummy data. You’ll see a red placeholder image.
    Nothing displayed: Confirm you open the link in your browser or the inline UI in Colab. If minimal Gradio tests worked, you should see something here.
    Large Dataset: This snippet is minimal—if you have thousands of images, scrolling one by one might be slow.

With this approach, you get a simple, single-item labeling workflow—one image and text field per screen, plus easy forward/back navigation. Enjoy!
You said:
This example is showing just red rectangles for all word images
Reasoned for a couple of seconds

If you see red placeholder images in place of your actual crops, it means the code can’t find the real image files on disk. By default, this snippet creates a red rectangle with:

Image.new("RGB", (300, 100), color="red")

whenever it fails to locate the requested file. Below are the usual reasons and how to fix them:
1. Check the File Paths
1.1 Does Your CSV Match the Actual Filenames?

    Your CSV has crop_filename values like word_001.png, but perhaps your actual file is named Word_001.PNG (capital letters or different extension), or it’s spelled differently (e.g., word1.png vs. word_1.png).
    Filenames are case-sensitive in many environments. If your CSV says word_1.png but the file is actually Word_1.png, Python/OpenCV/PIL won’t find it.

Solution: Make sure the exact string in your CSV crop_filename column matches the real file name in the folder, including correct case and extension.
1.2 Check the Folder Path

    By default, the code does:

    Then checks if os.path.exists(img_path): ...
    If your images aren’t in a subfolder named word_crops/, or if IMAGE_FOLDER is defined incorrectly, the path won’t match.

Solution: Ensure IMAGE_FOLDER is the correct directory name:

IMAGE_FOLDER = "word_crops"

And that your images are actually in, say, ./word_crops/word_001.png.
1.3 Using Absolute vs. Relative Paths

    If you’re on Colab, confirm that you uploaded the folder to the correct place. If your code is in /content/, then word_crops must also be in /content/.

    Sometimes you need an absolute path, e.g., /content/word_crops/.

    Double-check by printing:

    print("IMAGE_FOLDER =", IMAGE_FOLDER)
    print("sample filename =", samples[0]["filename"])
    img_path = os.path.join(IMAGE_FOLDER, samples[0]["filename"])
    print("Full path =", img_path, "Exists?", os.path.exists(img_path))

2. Confirm the CSV is Loading

If your CSV is empty or has unexpected columns, the snippet might create dummy placeholders. Look at the logs for:

Warning: recognized_words.csv not found, generating X dummy entries.

If you see that, you’re not actually using your real CSV.

Solution: Make sure recognized_words.csv is truly present, spelled correctly, and has the columns crop_filename, recognized_text.
3. Quick Debugging Tips

!ls word_crops

to confirm your PNG files are really there.
Check a single path:

    Case Sensitivity: If you’re on Windows (case-insensitive) it might work, but on Linux-based systems (including Colab) it will fail if the cases differ.

4. Summary

A red rectangle indicates the script couldn’t open the expected image file. Usually, one of these is wrong:

    Filename in CSV doesn’t exactly match the real file name.

Fix those path/filename issues, and you should see your actual word images instead of the red placeholders.
You said:
In my notebook, a gradio UI is rendered inline. Can I make it taller?
Reasoned for 9 seconds

By default, Gradio will render an inline UI in a Colab output cell that can be fairly short, causing scrollbars for taller interfaces. You have a few ways to make it taller:
1) Pass a height Parameter to launch()

In many Gradio versions (particularly for Interface and some Blocks setups), you can specify a height argument when calling launch(). For example:

demo.launch(height=1000)

This tells Gradio to allocate a taller iframe (1000px high) in the Colab output cell. Adjust the number as needed. For example, demo.launch(height=800) or demo.launch(height=1200).

    Note: In very recent Gradio releases, the height parameter may be deprecated for certain Blocks configurations, but it still works for standard Interface or many Blocks setups.

2) Colab Settings to Expand the Output Cell

Even if you set height, Google Colab sometimes clips large outputs by default. To fix that:

    Click the three dots in the upper-right corner of the output cell.
    Choose “Expand output” or “Enable scrolling” to show more vertical space.

Alternatively, under Tools → Settings → Editor (or Notebook in some Colab versions), you can adjust how large outputs can be before scrolling.
3) Use a Custom HTML / IFrame in Colab

If you find the height parameter is not recognized (in some advanced Blocks setups), you can embed the Gradio app in a manually sized iframe:

    Launch Gradio with demo.launch(share=True) to get a public URL (or a local URL).

    Manually create an <iframe> in the output cell. For example:

    from IPython.display import HTML

    public_url = demo.launch(share=True)[0]
    iframe_html = f"""
    <iframe src="{public_url}" style="width:100%; height:1000px; border:none;"></iframe>
    """
    display(HTML(iframe_html))

This forces an iframe of 1000px height in your Colab cell, regardless of Gradio’s default sizing.
4) Use Colab’s “Pro” or “Wide” Layout

Some Colab notebook themes or add-ons let you toggle a wide or full-screen layout, giving more horizontal space. This doesn’t always affect vertical height, but can help overall visibility.
5) Summary

    Try demo.launch(height=...) first if your Gradio version supports it.
    Check “Expand output” or “Disable scrolling” in the output cell if Colab still clips it.
    For full control, manually embed the Gradio app with a custom iframe.

With these options, you can see a taller, more comfortable Gradio interface inline in your Colab notebook.
You said:
Okay, now I have recognized_words_labelled.csv with my corrections. How can I go about fine tuning TrOCR with this dataset?
Reasoned for a second

Below is a step-by-step guide to fine-tune TrOCR (Transformer OCR) on your handwriting dataset—the dataset you just finished labeling in recognized_words_labelled.csv. We'll use the Hugging Face Transformers library in Python. The workflow involves:

    Preparing a dataset (images + corrected text).
    Defining a Dataset or a DatasetDict for train/validation splits.
    Using a TrOCR model + processor from Hugging Face (e.g., microsoft/trocr-base-handwritten).
    Fine-tuning via the Trainer or a custom training loop.

1. Install Dependencies

!pip install transformers datasets sentencepiece torchvision pillow

    transformers: for the TrOCR model.
    datasets: to handle your labeled data in a nice format.
    sentencepiece: needed for some HF tokenizers.
    torchvision + pillow: for image handling.

2. Organize Your Data

You have recognized_words_labelled.csv containing lines like:

crop_filename,recognized_text
word_001.png,HELLO
word_002.png,TEST
...

And a folder, say word_crops/, holding the images. A typical approach:

    Split your dataset into train/val (and possibly test). For instance, put 90% of the lines in train, 10% in val.
    Each row has (image_path, text_label).

Example:

data/
  train/
    word_0001.png
    word_0002.png
    ...
  val/
    word_0101.png
    ...
train_labels.csv
val_labels.csv

Or, keep them all in one folder word_crops/, but split the CSV into train_labels.csv and val_labels.csv. Your call.

For simplicity, let's assume one CSV with a “split” column indicating train or val. Or you can do the split programmatically.
3. Create a Hugging Face Dataset
3.1 Minimal Approach

You can directly build a Dataset from your CSV:

import pandas as pd
from datasets import Dataset

df = pd.read_csv("train_labels.csv")  # or recognized_words_labelled.csv
# Make sure you have columns ["crop_filename", "recognized_text"]
train_dataset = Dataset.from_pandas(df)

Do the same for a validation CSV if you have one:

df_val = pd.read_csv("val_labels.csv")
val_dataset = Dataset.from_pandas(df_val)

Then assemble into a DatasetDict:

from datasets import DatasetDict

dataset = DatasetDict({
    "train": train_dataset,
    "validation": val_dataset
})
print(dataset)

3.2 Each Row Format

You want each row to look like:

{
  "crop_filename": "word_001.png",
  "recognized_text": "HELLO"
}

Or whatever your columns are named.

If your CSV has extra columns, you can rename or drop them to match your desired schema.
4. Load a Pretrained TrOCR Model & Processor

We’ll pick microsoft/trocr-base-handwritten, which is pretrained on the IAM handwriting dataset. Feel free to try the “large” variant or other TroCR flavors.

import torch
device = "cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu"

from transformers import TrOCRProcessor, VisionEncoderDecoderModel

processor = TrOCRProcessor.from_pretrained("microsoft/trocr-base-handwritten")
model = VisionEncoderDecoderModel.from_pretrained("microsoft/trocr-base-handwritten").to(device)

5. Preprocessing Function

We need to:

    Load the image from crop_filename.
    Convert it to RGB (TrOCR expects RGB).
    Call processor(images=..., text=...) to get pixel_values and labels.

import os
from PIL import Image

def preprocess_examples(examples):
    images = []
    texts = []
    for path, text in zip(examples["crop_filename"], examples["recognized_text"]):
        # Load image
        image_path = os.path.join("word_crops", path)  # adjust if needed
        img = Image.open(image_path).convert("RGB")
        
        images.append(img)
        texts.append(text)

    # Use the TrOCR processor
    model_inputs = processor(
        images=images, 
        text=texts,
        return_tensors="pt", 
        padding=True
    )
    
    # We need to return a dict with the same keys the Trainer expects
    batch = {}
    batch["pixel_values"] = model_inputs["pixel_values"]
    batch["labels"] = model_inputs["labels"]
    return batch

You can see we do:

    images=img: the loaded PIL image.
    text=texts: the ground-truth text for that image.
    Return pixel_values & labels as PyTorch tensors.

6. Apply the Preprocessing to the Dataset

We map this function to both train and val subsets:

train_dataset = dataset["train"].map(
    preprocess_examples,
    batched=True,
    remove_columns=["crop_filename", "recognized_text"]
)

val_dataset = dataset["validation"].map(
    preprocess_examples,
    batched=True,
    remove_columns=["crop_filename", "recognized_text"]
)

Now each dataset entry has keys like pixel_values and labels.
Check or Resize

If you want to resize images systematically (e.g., all to 224×224) or do data augmentation (random crop, etc.), do so inside preprocess_examples or via a separate function. Currently, we rely on the default resizing within the TrOCR processor.
7. Set Up a Trainer

We can use Seq2SeqTrainer (since it’s effectively an encoder-decoder model). Alternatively, we can do a standard Trainer because VisionEncoderDecoderModel is used similarly.

from transformers import Seq2SeqTrainingArguments, Seq2SeqTrainer

training_args = Seq2SeqTrainingArguments(
    output_dir="./trocr-finetune",
    num_train_epochs=10,
    per_device_train_batch_size=2,
    per_device_eval_batch_size=2,
    evaluation_strategy="steps",
    save_steps=200,
    logging_steps=100,
    eval_steps=200,
    predict_with_generate=True,  # for validation text generation
    fp16=True if device=="cuda" else False,  # use 16-bit if GPU
)

def compute_metrics(eval_pred):
    # Usually you do character/word error rate
    # For simplicity, let's skip or do a placeholder
    return {}

trainer = Seq2SeqTrainer(
    model=model,
    args=training_args,
    train_dataset=train_dataset,
    eval_dataset=val_dataset,
    tokenizer=processor.feature_extractor,  # or pass entire processor if older HF ver
    compute_metrics=compute_metrics
)

    train_dataset & eval_dataset are the mapped versions that have pixel_values, labels.
    Adjust num_train_epochs, save_steps, and batch_size as appropriate.
    If your GPU is limited in memory, keep batch_size small (1 or 2).
    If you want to measure accuracy or CER, you’d define a real compute_metrics. For now, we can skip it or do a placeholder.

8. Training

trainer.train()

This will:

    Load each (pixel_values, labels) batch from train_dataset.
    Forward/backprop the model.
    Evaluate on val_dataset at intervals (if evaluation_strategy="steps").

Checkpoints are saved in ./trocr-finetune/checkpoint-XXXX and the best model is usually in the last or a “best” checkpoint depending on settings.
9. Evaluating & Testing Inference

After training completes, you can do a quick test:

model.eval()

test_image = Image.open("word_crops/word_0100.png").convert("RGB")
pixel_values = processor(images=test_image, return_tensors="pt").pixel_values.to(device)

generated_ids = model.generate(pixel_values)
pred_text = processor.batch_decode(generated_ids, skip_special_tokens=True)[0]
print("Predicted text:", pred_text)

If fine-tuning succeeded, it should produce text matching your handwriting style more accurately.
10. Key Tips

    Data Size: With 200–300 or fewer images, you may overfit quickly; consider more data or heavy augmentation.
    Batch Size: TrOCR can be memory-intensive; keep batch size small.
    Learning Rate: The default might be too high or low. Try adjusting in Seq2SeqTrainingArguments (e.g., learning_rate=1e-4) or use a scheduler.
    Metrics: Real handwriting tasks typically measure character error rate (CER) or word error rate (WER). You can implement that by comparing predicted vs. ground-truth strings after each eval step.
    Epoch Count: Start with ~10 epochs, watch if the model overfits. Adjust as needed.
    Check GPU: Make sure you have a GPU environment (device=="cuda"). Fine-tuning on CPU will be very slow.

11. Summary

    Create a CSV or multiple CSVs with (crop_filename, recognized_text).
    Load them into a DatasetDict with train/val splits.
    Map a function that loads images, uses the TrOCR processor, returning pixel_values & labels.
    Fine-tune with Seq2SeqTrainer.
    Test the resulting model on a few images to confirm improvements.

By following these steps, you’ll fine-tune TrOCR on your personal handwriting style using the corrected labels in recognized_words_labelled.csv and the corresponding images. Good luck!
You said:
Can you provide me with some code that splits recognized_words_labeled.csv into suitable sets for train_labels.csv and val_labels.csv?
Reasoned for a few seconds

Below is a minimal Python snippet that takes your recognized_words_labeled.csv and splits it into two files—train_labels.csv and val_labels.csv. By default, this example does a 90/10 split, but you can customize the ratio as needed.

import pandas as pd

# 1) Read your labeled CSV
df = pd.read_csv("recognized_words_labeled.csv")  # adjust file name if needed

# 2) Shuffle the rows (so train/val sets are random)
df = df.sample(frac=1, random_state=42).reset_index(drop=True)

# 3) Compute split index (90% train, 10% val)
train_frac = 0.9
train_size = int(train_frac * len(df))

# 4) Subset the rows
train_df = df.iloc[:train_size]
val_df = df.iloc[train_size:]

# 5) Write out each subset
train_df.to_csv("train_labels.csv", index=False)
val_df.to_csv("val_labels.csv", index=False)

print(f"Train set: {len(train_df)} rows, Val set: {len(val_df)} rows")

How It Works

    Shuffle: df.sample(frac=1, random_state=42) randomizes row order so that the first train_size fraction is a representative sample of the whole dataset.
    Split: 90% for training, 10% for validation. Adjust train_frac = 0.8 or 0.85 etc. if you want a different split.
    Save: Writes out two new CSV files: train_labels.csv and val_labels.csv. Both have the same columns as the original.

These CSVs are now suitable for typical ML usage, where you train on train_labels.csv and evaluate on val_labels.csv. If you also need a test set, you can do a 3-way split by further splitting val_df.
You said:
Trying to run the training, I get a prompt to Logging into wandb.ai. Is this necessary?
Reasoned for a second

You don’t need to log into Weights & Biases (wandb.ai) to train your model. By default, some Hugging Face training examples (or certain environment configurations) might automatically enable W&B integration. If you prefer not to use W&B, you can disable it in one of these ways:
1. Disable W&B by Setting report_to

When you create or modify your TrainingArguments (or Seq2SeqTrainingArguments), set:

training_args = Seq2SeqTrainingArguments(
    output_dir="./trocr-finetune",
    num_train_epochs=10,
    per_device_train_batch_size=2,
    evaluation_strategy="steps",
    logging_steps=100,
    save_steps=200,
    # ...
    report_to="none"  # <--- This disables W&B
)

Or pass a list if you have multiple reporting backends:

training_args = Seq2SeqTrainingArguments(
    # ...
    report_to=[]
)

Either report_to="none" or report_to=[] ensures that no W&B integration is launched.
2. Disable W&B via Environment Variable

If you don’t want to edit code, you can define an env variable before running training:

export WANDB_DISABLED=true

or in Python:

import os
os.environ["WANDB_DISABLED"] = "true"

When WANDB_DISABLED=true, the Hugging Face Trainer won’t attempt to log to wandb.
3. Remove WandB from Requirements

If you never installed wandb, you generally won’t see the wandb login prompt. If you installed wandb (or are on a system that has it preinstalled), the Trainer may try to use it by default unless you disable it. In a custom environment you can also uninstall:

pip uninstall wandb

But typically using report_to="none" or WANDB_DISABLED=true is enough.
4. Conclusion

    No, it’s not necessary to use W&B for training.
    Yes, you can simply turn it off with report_to="none", report_to=[], or by setting the WANDB_DISABLED environment variable. Once disabled, you won’t get the login prompt and can train locally without any external logging.

You said:
When I tried running trainer.train(), I got this error: TypeError: ViTModel.forward() got an unexpected keyword argument 'num_items_in_batch'

Analyzing the error

I'm examining the issue with the ViTModel's forward method, considering potential causes like improper model calling, unexpected data preprocessing arguments, or Transformers version incompatibility.

Ensuring correct preprocessing

I’m detailing that the preprocessing and dataset mapping must be accurate. Special attention is given to the preprocess_examples function, as it might be returning unexpected keys or incorrect data formats.

Investigating potential issues

I’m considering various problems like mismatches between the model and processor, incorrect preprocess function outputs, or 'num_items_in_batch' being unexpectedly passed. I’ll update when more details are available.

Rectifying dataset issues

The issue might stem from the Trainer passing extra columns to the model, which causes errors. The solution is to ensure the dataset only includes the needed columns: pixel_values and labels.

Ensuring dataset accuracy

Mapping the preprocess function must focus on removing columns not used by the model. This approach minimizes errors like 'num_items_in_batch' being passed incorrectly.

Verifying data parameters

I’m confirming the model's input requirements, ensuring the dataset's columns match the expected 'pixel_values' and 'labels'. This involves examining the preprocess function and using remove_columns correctly.

Identifying column errors

I’m analyzing 'num_items_in_batch' being passed with preprocessing. Ensuring train and validation datasets include only 'pixel_values' and 'labels' is crucial, and tokenization of labels should also be verified.

The error message you're encountering:

TypeError: ViTModel.forward() got an unexpected keyword argument 'num_items_in_batch'

indicates that an unexpected keyword argument (num_items_in_batch) is being passed to the ViTModel's forward method. This typically arises when extra columns are present in your dataset that the model doesn't expect. The Hugging Face Trainer or Seq2SeqTrainer tries to pass all columns in the dataset to the model as keyword arguments. If any of these columns are not recognized by the model, it results in such errors.

To resolve this, you need to ensure that your datasets (train_dataset and val_dataset) contain only the columns that the model expects: pixel_values and labels.

Here's a comprehensive guide to help you debug and fix this issue:
1. Verify Your Preprocessing Function

Ensure that your preprocessing function only returns the necessary fields (pixel_values and labels) and excludes all other columns.
Example Preprocessing Function

import os
from PIL import Image
from transformers import TrOCRProcessor

processor = TrOCRProcessor.from_pretrained("microsoft/trocr-base-handwritten")

def preprocess_examples(examples):
    images = []
    texts = []
    for path, text in zip(examples["crop_filename"], examples["recognized_text"]):
        image_path = os.path.join("word_crops", path)  # Adjust if your folder is named differently
        try:
            img = Image.open(image_path).convert("RGB")
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"Error loading image {image_path}: {e}")
            img = Image.new("RGB", (300, 100), color="red")  # Placeholder for missing images
        
        images.append(img)
        texts.append(text)
    
    # Process images and texts
    model_inputs = processor(
        images=images,
        text=texts,
        return_tensors="pt",
        padding=True,
        truncation=True
    )
    
    # Return only the fields required by the model
    batch = {
        "pixel_values": model_inputs["pixel_values"],
        "labels": model_inputs["labels"]
    }
    return batch

Key Points:

    Load and Convert Images: Ensure images are correctly loaded and converted to RGB.
    Processor Usage: The TrOCRProcessor handles both image and text preprocessing.
    Return Only Necessary Fields: The function returns a dictionary with only pixel_values and labels.

2. Ensure Proper Column Removal

When mapping the preprocessing function to your dataset, remove all unnecessary columns to prevent them from being passed to the model.
Example Mapping with Column Removal

from datasets import DatasetDict

# Assuming you've already loaded and split your dataset into train and validation
# For example:
# dataset = DatasetDict({
#     "train": train_dataset,
#     "validation": val_dataset
# })

# Apply the preprocessing
processed_dataset = dataset.map(
    preprocess_examples,
    batched=True,
    remove_columns=["crop_filename", "recognized_text"]  # Remove original columns
)

# Verify the columns
print("Train Dataset Columns:", processed_dataset["train"].column_names)
print("Validation Dataset Columns:", processed_dataset["validation"].column_names)

Key Points:

    batched=True: Processes multiple examples at once for efficiency.
    remove_columns: Ensures that only the necessary columns (pixel_values and labels) remain.
    Verification: Printing the column names helps confirm that unwanted columns are removed.

3. Inspect Your Datasets

Before proceeding to training, inspect your datasets to ensure they contain only the required columns.
Example Inspection

# Inspect a few examples from the training set
print(processed_dataset["train"][0])
print(processed_dataset["train"][1])

# Inspect a few examples from the validation set
print(processed_dataset["validation"][0])
print(processed_dataset["validation"][1])

Expected Output:

Each example should only have pixel_values and labels:

{
    'pixel_values': tensor([...]),
    'labels': tensor([...])
}

If you see any additional keys (like crop_filename, num_items_in_batch, etc.), that's a sign that extra columns are still present.
4. Adjust Training Arguments to Disable Weights & Biases (W&B) Logging

Since you previously encountered issues with W&B logging prompts, ensure that W&B is disabled to prevent unnecessary prompts and potential conflicts.
Example Training Arguments with W&B Disabled

from transformers import Seq2SeqTrainingArguments

training_args = Seq2SeqTrainingArguments(
    output_dir="./trocr-finetune",
    num_train_epochs=10,
    per_device_train_batch_size=2,
    per_device_eval_batch_size=2,
    evaluation_strategy="steps",
    save_steps=200,
    logging_steps=100,
    eval_steps=200,
    predict_with_generate=True,
    fp16=torch.cuda.is_available(),
    report_to="none",  # Disable W&B
)

5. Define the Trainer Correctly

Ensure that the Trainer or Seq2SeqTrainer is correctly set up without any conflicts.
Example Trainer Setup

from transformers import Seq2SeqTrainer, Seq2SeqTrainingArguments, TrOCRProcessor, VisionEncoderDecoderModel

# Initialize the processor and model
processor = TrOCRProcessor.from_pretrained("microsoft/trocr-base-handwritten")
model = VisionEncoderDecoderModel.from_pretrained("microsoft/trocr-base-handwritten").to(device)

# Define training arguments (as above)
training_args = Seq2SeqTrainingArguments(
    output_dir="./trocr-finetune",
    num_train_epochs=10,
    per_device_train_batch_size=2,
    per_device_eval_batch_size=2,
    evaluation_strategy="steps",
    save_steps=200,
    logging_steps=100,
    eval_steps=200,
    predict_with_generate=True,
    fp16=torch.cuda.is_available(),
    report_to="none",  # Disable W&B
)

# Initialize the Trainer
trainer = Seq2SeqTrainer(
    model=model,
    args=training_args,
    train_dataset=processed_dataset["train"],
    eval_dataset=processed_dataset["validation"],
    tokenizer=processor,
    # compute_metrics can be defined if you have specific metrics
)

# Start training
trainer.train()

Key Points:

    tokenizer=processor: Passing the entire processor ensures both image and text processing are handled.
    compute_metrics: Optional but recommended for monitoring training progress (e.g., CER or WER).

6. Additional Debugging Steps

If the issue persists after ensuring the dataset is correctly formatted, consider the following debugging steps:
6.1. Inspect the Dataset Columns Again

print("Train Dataset Columns:", processed_dataset["train"].column_names)
print("Validation Dataset Columns:", processed_dataset["validation"].column_names)

Ensure that only pixel_values and labels are present.
6.2. Check for Hidden Columns

Sometimes, datasets might carry hidden columns or metadata. To remove all non-essential columns, adjust the remove_columns parameter accordingly.

all_columns = processed_dataset["train"].column_names
essential_columns = ["pixel_values", "labels"]
columns_to_remove = [col for col in all_columns if col not in essential_columns]

processed_dataset = dataset.map(
    preprocess_examples,
    batched=True,
    remove_columns=columns_to_remove
)

6.3. Ensure Correct Processor and Model Pairing

Ensure that you're using the compatible processor and model. For TrOCR, it's essential to use TrOCRProcessor with VisionEncoderDecoderModel.
6.4. Update Hugging Face Libraries

Ensure that your transformers and datasets libraries are up-to-date to avoid compatibility issues.

pip install --upgrade transformers datasets

7. Complete Example Workflow

Here's a complete workflow incorporating all the steps above to fine-tune TrOCR using your labeled dataset.
Step 1: Install and Import Dependencies

!pip install --upgrade transformers datasets sentencepiece torchvision pillow

import pandas as pd
import torch
from datasets import Dataset, DatasetDict
from transformers import TrOCRProcessor, VisionEncoderDecoderModel, Seq2SeqTrainer, Seq2SeqTrainingArguments
from PIL import Image
import os

Step 2: Split the CSV into Train and Validation Sets

Assuming you've already split recognized_words_labelled.csv into train_labels.csv and val_labels.csv as per your earlier step.

# Read CSVs
train_df = pd.read_csv("train_labels.csv")
val_df = pd.read_csv("val_labels.csv")

# Convert to Hugging Face Datasets
train_dataset = Dataset.from_pandas(train_df)
val_dataset = Dataset.from_pandas(val_df)

# Create a DatasetDict
dataset = DatasetDict({
    "train": train_dataset,
    "validation": val_dataset
})

Step 3: Initialize Processor and Model

processor = TrOCRProcessor.from_pretrained("microsoft/trocr-base-handwritten")
model = VisionEncoderDecoderModel.from_pretrained("microsoft/trocr-base-handwritten")

device = "cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu"
model.to(device)

Step 4: Define the Preprocessing Function

def preprocess_examples(examples):
    images = []
    texts = []
    for path, text in zip(examples["crop_filename"], examples["recognized_text"]):
        image_path = os.path.join("word_crops", path)  # Adjust if your folder is named differently
        try:
            img = Image.open(image_path).convert("RGB")
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"Error loading image {image_path}: {e}")
            img = Image.new("RGB", (300, 100), color="red")  # Placeholder for missing images
        
        images.append(img)
        texts.append(text)
    
    # Process images and texts
    model_inputs = processor(
        images=images,
        text=texts,
        return_tensors="pt",
        padding=True,
        truncation=True
    )
    
    batch = {

Apologies for the continued issues you're experiencing. The error message:

TypeError: ViTModel.forward() got an unexpected keyword argument 'num_items_in_batch'

suggests that an unexpected keyword argument (num_items_in_batch) is being passed to the ViTModel's forward method. This typically occurs when extra columns are present in your dataset that the model doesn't expect. The Hugging Face Trainer or Seq2SeqTrainer tries to pass all columns in the dataset to the model as keyword arguments. If any of these columns are not recognized by the model, it results in such errors.

To resolve this, you need to ensure that your datasets (train_dataset and val_dataset) contain only the columns that the model expects: pixel_values and labels. Below is a comprehensive, step-by-step guide to help you fine-tune TrOCR without encountering this error.
Step-by-Step Guide to Fine-Tune TrOCR
1. Install Required Dependencies

Ensure you have the latest versions of the necessary libraries to avoid compatibility issues.

pip install --upgrade transformers datasets sentencepiece torchvision pillow

2. Split Your Labeled CSV into Train and Validation Sets

Assuming you have recognized_words_labeled.csv, we'll split it into train_labels.csv and val_labels.csv with a 90/10 split.

import pandas as pd

# Read the labeled CSV
df = pd.read_csv("recognized_words_labeled.csv")

# Shuffle the DataFrame
df = df.sample(frac=1, random_state=42).reset_index(drop=True)

# Define the split ratio
train_frac = 0.9
train_size = int(train_frac * len(df))

# Split the DataFrame
train_df = df.iloc[:train_size]
val_df = df.iloc[train_size:]

# Save the splits to new CSV files
train_df.to_csv("train_labels.csv", index=False)
val_df.to_csv("val_labels.csv", index=False)

print(f"Train set: {len(train_df)} rows")
print(f"Validation set: {len(val_df)} rows")

3. Prepare Your Dataset with Hugging Face Datasets

We'll load the CSV files into Hugging Face Datasets and preprocess them to ensure only the required columns are present.

import pandas as pd
from datasets import Dataset, DatasetDict

# Load the CSV files into pandas DataFrames
train_df = pd.read_csv("train_labels.csv")
val_df = pd.read_csv("val_labels.csv")

# Convert pandas DataFrames to Hugging Face Datasets
train_dataset = Dataset.from_pandas(train_df)
val_dataset = Dataset.from_pandas(val_df)

# Create a DatasetDict
dataset = DatasetDict({
    "train": train_dataset,
    "validation": val_dataset
})

print(dataset)

4. Initialize the TrOCR Processor and Model

We'll use the TrOCRProcessor and VisionEncoderDecoderModel from Hugging Face.

import torch
from transformers import TrOCRProcessor, VisionEncoderDecoderModel

# Check if GPU is available
device = "cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu"
print(f"Using device: {device}")

# Initialize the processor and model
processor = TrOCRProcessor.from_pretrained("microsoft/trocr-base-handwritten")
model = VisionEncoderDecoderModel.from_pretrained("microsoft/trocr-base-handwritten")
model.to(device)

5. Define the Preprocessing Function

This function will:

    Load and preprocess images.
    Tokenize the texts.
    Ensure only pixel_values and labels are returned.

import os
from PIL import Image

def preprocess_examples(examples):
    images = []
    texts = []
    for path, text in zip(examples["crop_filename"], examples["recognized_text"]):
        image_path = os.path.join("word_crops", path)  # Adjust if your folder is named differently
        try:
            img = Image.open(image_path).convert("RGB")
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"Error loading image {image_path}: {e}")
            img = Image.new("RGB", (300, 100), color="red")  # Placeholder for missing images
        
        images.append(img)
        texts.append(text)
    
    # Process images and texts
    model_inputs = processor(
        images=images,
        text=texts,
        return_tensors="pt",
        padding=True,
        truncation=True
    )
    
    # Return only the fields required by the model
    batch = {
        "pixel_values": model_inputs["pixel_values"],
        "labels": model_inputs["labels"]
    }
    return batch

6. Apply the Preprocessing and Remove Unnecessary Columns

We need to map the preprocessing function to the datasets and ensure that only pixel_values and labels are retained.

# Apply the preprocessing to the training set
processed_train = dataset["train"].map(
    preprocess_examples,
    batched=True,
    remove_columns=["crop_filename", "recognized_text"]
)

# Apply the preprocessing to the validation set
processed_val = dataset["validation"].map(
    preprocess_examples,
    batched=True,
    remove_columns=["crop_filename", "recognized_text"]
)

# Create a new DatasetDict with processed datasets
processed_dataset = DatasetDict({
    "train": processed_train,
    "validation": processed_val
})

# Verify the columns
print("Train Dataset Columns:", processed_dataset["train"].column_names)
print("Validation Dataset Columns:", processed_dataset["validation"].column_names)

Expected Output:

Train Dataset Columns: ['pixel_values', 'labels']
Validation Dataset Columns: ['pixel_values', 'labels']

If you see any additional columns, adjust the remove_columns parameter accordingly to ensure only pixel_values and labels are present.
7. Define Training Arguments with W&B Disabled

We'll set up Seq2SeqTrainingArguments and disable Weights & Biases (W&B) logging to avoid unnecessary prompts.

from transformers import Seq2SeqTrainingArguments

training_args = Seq2SeqTrainingArguments(
    output_dir="./trocr-finetune",
    num_train_epochs=10,
    per_device_train_batch_size=2,
    per_device_eval_batch_size=2,
    evaluation_strategy="steps",
    save_steps=200,
    logging_steps=100,
    eval_steps=200,
    predict_with_generate=True,
    fp16=torch.cuda.is_available(),
    report_to="none",  # Disable Weights & Biases
    logging_dir='./logs',
)

8. Initialize the Trainer

We'll use Seq2SeqTrainer for fine-tuning.

from transformers import Seq2SeqTrainer

trainer = Seq2SeqTrainer(
    model=model,
    args=training_args,
    train_dataset=processed_dataset["train"],
    eval_dataset=processed_dataset["validation"],
    tokenizer=processor,  # Pass the processor which handles both image and text
    # You can define compute_metrics here if desired
)

9. Start Training

Begin the fine-tuning process.

trainer.train()

G. Final Comprehensive Example

Below is a complete, self-contained example that integrates all the above steps. This should help you fine-tune TrOCR without encountering the TypeError.

# 1. Install Dependencies
!pip install --upgrade transformers datasets sentencepiece torchvision pillow

# 2. Import Libraries
import pandas as pd
from datasets import Dataset, DatasetDict
from transformers import TrOCRProcessor, VisionEncoderDecoderModel, Seq2SeqTrainer, Seq2SeqTrainingArguments
from PIL import Image
import torch
import os

# 3. Split the CSV into Train and Validation Sets (if not already done)
# If you've already split, skip this step
df = pd.read_csv("recognized_words_labeled.csv")
df = df.sample(frac=1, random_state=42).reset_index(drop=True)
train_frac = 0.9
train_size = int(train_frac * len(df))
train_df = df.iloc[:train_size]
val_df = df.iloc[train_size:]
train_df.to_csv("train_labels.csv", index=False)
val_df.to_csv("val_labels.csv", index=False)
print(f"Train set: {len(train_df)} rows")
print(f"Validation set: {len(val_df)} rows")

# 4. Load Datasets
train_df = pd.read_csv("train_labels.csv")
val_df = pd.read_csv("val_labels.csv")
train_dataset = Dataset.from_pandas(train_df)
val_dataset = Dataset.from_pandas(val_df)
dataset = DatasetDict({
    "train": train_dataset,
    "validation": val_dataset
})
print(dataset)

# 5. Initialize Processor and Model
device = "cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu"
print(f"Using device: {device}")

processor = TrOCRProcessor.from_pretrained("microsoft/trocr-base-handwritten")
model = VisionEncoderDecoderModel.from_pretrained("microsoft/trocr-base-handwritten")
model.to(device)

# 6. Define Preprocessing Function
def preprocess_examples(examples):
    images = []
    texts = []
    for path, text in zip(examples["crop_filename"], examples["recognized_text"]):
        image_path = os.path.join("word_crops", path)  # Adjust if your folder is named differently
        try:
            img = Image.open(image_path).convert("RGB")
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"Error loading image {image_path}: {e}")
            img = Image.new("RGB", (300, 100), color="red")  # Placeholder for missing images
        
        images.append(img)
        texts.append(text)
    
    # Process images and texts
    model_inputs = processor(
        images=images,
        text=texts,
        return_tensors="pt",
        padding=True,
        truncation=True
    )
    
    # Return only the fields required by the model
    batch = {
        "pixel_values": model_inputs["pixel_values"],
        "labels": model_inputs["labels"]
    }
    return batch

# 7. Apply Preprocessing and Remove Unnecessary Columns
processed_train = dataset["train"].map(
    preprocess_examples,
    batched=True,
    remove_columns=["crop_filename", "recognized_text"]
)

processed_val = dataset["validation"].map(
    preprocess_examples,
    batched=True,
    remove_columns=["crop_filename", "recognized_text"]
)

processed_dataset = DatasetDict({
    "train": processed_train,
    "validation": processed_val
})

# Verify the columns
print("Train Dataset Columns:", processed_dataset["train"].column_names)
print("Validation Dataset Columns:", processed_dataset["validation"].column_names)

# 8. Define Training Arguments with W&B Disabled
training_args = Seq2SeqTrainingArguments(
    output_dir="./trocr-finetune",
    num_train_epochs=10,
    per_device_train_batch_size=2,
    per_device_eval_batch_size=2,
    evaluation_strategy="steps",
    save_steps=200,
    logging_steps=100,
    eval_steps=200,
    predict_with_generate=True,
    fp16=torch.cuda.is_available(),
    report_to="none",  # Disable Weights & Biases
    logging_dir='./logs',
)

# 9. Initialize the Trainer
trainer = Seq2SeqTrainer(
    model=model,
    args=training_args,
    train_dataset=processed_dataset["train"],
    eval_dataset=processed_dataset["validation"],
    tokenizer=processor,  # Pass the processor which handles both image and text
    # compute_metrics=compute_metrics,  # Optional: Define if you have specific metrics
)

# 10. Start Training
trainer.train()

Explanation of the Comprehensive Example

    Dependencies Installation: Ensure all necessary libraries are installed and updated.
    Import Libraries: Import all required Python libraries.
    CSV Splitting: Split the labeled CSV into training and validation sets with a 90/10 ratio. Skip this step if already done.
    Load Datasets: Load the split CSVs into Hugging Face Datasets and create a DatasetDict.
    Initialize Processor and Model: Load the TrOCRProcessor and VisionEncoderDecoderModel from Hugging Face and move the model to the appropriate device (GPU/CPU).
    Preprocessing Function: Define a function to preprocess images and texts, ensuring only pixel_values and labels are returned.
    Apply Preprocessing: Map the preprocessing function to both training and validation datasets, removing all other columns.
    Training Arguments: Define Seq2SeqTrainingArguments, disabling W&B by setting report_to="none".
    Initialize Trainer: Create a Seq2SeqTrainer instance with the model, training arguments, and datasets.
    Start Training: Begin the fine-tuning process.

def preprocess_examples(examples):
    images = []
    texts = []
    for path, text in zip(examples["crop_filename"], examples["recognized_text"]):
        image_path = os.path.join("word_crops", path)
        try:
            img = Image.open(image_path).convert("RGB")
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"Error loading image {image_path}: {e}")
            img = Image.new("RGB", (300, 100), color="red")
        
        images.append(img)
        texts.append(text)
    
    # Process images and texts
    model_inputs = processor(
        images=images,
        text=texts,
        return_tensors="pt",
        padding=True,
        truncation=True
    )
    
    # Convert tensors to lists
    batch = {
        "pixel_values": model_inputs["pixel_values"].tolist(),
        "labels": model_inputs["labels"].tolist()
    }
    return batch

Key Changes:

    Convert Tensors to Lists: By using .tolist(), you convert PyTorch tensors to standard Python lists, which the datasets library can handle correctly.

    Ensure Only Required Fields Are Returned: The function should return only pixel_values and labels.

2. Apply the Preprocessing Function Correctly

When mapping the preprocessing function to your dataset, ensure that all unnecessary columns are removed. This prevents any extra data from being inadvertently passed to the model.
Mapping Function:

from datasets import DatasetDict

# Apply the preprocessing to the training set
processed_train = dataset["train"].map(
    preprocess_examples,
    batched=True,
    remove_columns=["crop_filename", "recognized_text"]  # Remove original columns
)

# Apply the preprocessing to the validation set
processed_val = dataset["validation"].map(
    preprocess_examples,
    batched=True,
    remove_columns=["crop_filename", "recognized_text"]  # Remove original columns
)

# Create a new DatasetDict with processed datasets
processed_dataset = DatasetDict({
    "train": processed_train,
    "validation": processed_val
})

# Verify the columns
print("Train Dataset Columns:", processed_dataset["train"].column_names)
print("Validation Dataset Columns:", processed_dataset["validation"].column_names)

Expected Output:

Train Dataset Columns: ['pixel_values', 'labels']
Validation Dataset Columns: ['pixel_values', 'labels']

If you see any additional columns, revisit the remove_columns parameter to ensure all unwanted columns are excluded.
3. Set the Correct Dataset Format

After mapping, explicitly set the dataset format to PyTorch tensors. This helps ensure that the Trainer receives the data in the correct format.

processed_dataset.set_format(
    type="torch",
    columns=["pixel_values", "labels"]
)

4. Define Training Arguments Without W&B

As previously mentioned, disable Weights & Biases (W&B) to prevent unnecessary prompts.

from transformers import Seq2SeqTrainingArguments

training_args = Seq2SeqTrainingArguments(
    output_dir="./trocr-finetune",
    num_train_epochs=10,
    per_device_train_batch_size=2,
    per_device_eval_batch_size=2,
    evaluation_strategy="steps",
    save_steps=200,
    logging_steps=100,
    eval_steps=200,
    predict_with_generate=True,
    fp16=torch.cuda.is_available(),
    report_to="none",  # Disable Weights & Biases
    logging_dir='./logs',
)

5. Initialize the Trainer Correctly

Ensure that you pass the processor correctly and that the model is properly set up.

from transformers import Seq2SeqTrainer

trainer = Seq2SeqTrainer(
    model=model,
    args=training_args,
    train_dataset=processed_dataset["train"],
    eval_dataset=processed_dataset["validation"],
    tokenizer=processor,  # Pass the processor which handles both image and text
    # You can define compute_metrics here if desired
)

Note: If you have specific metrics (like Character Error Rate), you can define a compute_metrics function and pass it to the trainer.
6. Start the Training

Now, you should be able to start the training without encountering the TypeError.

trainer.train()

8. Complete Comprehensive Example

To provide clarity, here's a complete, step-by-step script integrating all the above corrections. This script should work seamlessly if all paths and filenames are correctly set.
Complete Script:

# 1. Install Dependencies
!pip install --upgrade transformers datasets sentencepiece torchvision pillow

# 2. Import Libraries
import pandas as pd
from datasets import Dataset, DatasetDict
from transformers import TrOCRProcessor, VisionEncoderDecoderModel, Seq2SeqTrainer, Seq2SeqTrainingArguments
from PIL import Image
import torch
import os

# 3. Split the CSV into Train and Validation Sets
# If you've already split, skip this step
df = pd.read_csv("recognized_words_labeled.csv")
df = df.sample(frac=1, random_state=42).reset_index(drop=True)
train_frac = 0.9
train_size = int(train_frac * len(df))
train_df = df.iloc[:train_size]
val_df = df.iloc[train_size:]
train_df.to_csv("train_labels.csv", index=False)
val_df.to_csv("val_labels.csv", index=False)
print(f"Train set: {len(train_df)} rows")
print(f"Validation set: {len(val_df)} rows")

# 4. Load Datasets
train_df = pd.read_csv("train_labels.csv")
val_df = pd.read_csv("val_labels.csv")
train_dataset = Dataset.from_pandas(train_df)
val_dataset = Dataset.from_pandas(val_df)
dataset = DatasetDict({
    "train": train_dataset,
    "validation": val_dataset
})
print(dataset)

# 5. Initialize Processor and Model
device = "cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu"
print(f"Using device: {device}")

processor = TrOCRProcessor.from_pretrained("microsoft/trocr-base-handwritten")
model = VisionEncoderDecoderModel.from_pretrained("microsoft/trocr-base-handwritten")
model.to(device)

# 6. Define Preprocessing Function
def preprocess_examples(examples):
    images = []
    texts = []
    for path, text in zip(examples["crop_filename"], examples["recognized_text"]):
        image_path = os.path.join("word_crops", path)  # Adjust if your folder is named differently
        try:
            img = Image.open(image_path).convert("RGB")
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"Error loading image {image_path}: {e}")
            img = Image.new("RGB", (300, 100), color="red")  # Placeholder for missing images
        
        images.append(img)
        texts.append(text)
    
    # Process images and texts
    model_inputs = processor(
        images=images,
        text=texts,
        return_tensors="pt",
        padding=True,
        truncation=True
    )
    
    # Convert tensors to lists
    batch = {
        "pixel_values": model_inputs["pixel_values"].tolist(),
        "labels": model_inputs["labels"].tolist()
    }
    return batch

# 7. Apply Preprocessing and Remove Unnecessary Columns
processed_train = dataset["train"].map(
    preprocess_examples,
    batched=True,
    remove_columns=["crop_filename", "recognized_text"]  # Remove original columns
)

processed_val = dataset["validation"].map(
    preprocess_examples,
    batched=True,
    remove_columns=["crop_filename", "recognized_text"]  # Remove original columns
)

processed_dataset = DatasetDict({
    "train": processed_train,
    "validation": processed_val
})

# 8. Set Dataset Format to PyTorch Tensors
processed_dataset.set_format(
    type="torch",
    columns=["pixel_values", "labels"]
)

# Verify the columns
print("Train Dataset Columns:", processed_dataset["train"].column_names)
print("Validation Dataset Columns:", processed_dataset["validation"].column_names)

# 9. Define Training Arguments with W&B Disabled
training_args = Seq2SeqTrainingArguments(
    output_dir="./trocr-finetune",
    num_train_epochs=10,
    per_device_train_batch_size=2,
    per_device_eval_batch_size=2,
    evaluation_strategy="steps",
    save_steps=200,
    logging_steps=100,
    eval_steps=200,
    predict_with_generate=True,
    fp16=torch.cuda.is_available(),
    report_to="none",  # Disable Weights & Biases
    logging_dir='./logs',
)

# 10. Initialize the Trainer
trainer = Seq2SeqTrainer(
    model=model,
    args=training_args,
    train_dataset=processed_dataset["train"],
    eval_dataset=processed_dataset["validation"],
    tokenizer=processor,  # Pass the processor which handles both image and text
    # compute_metrics=compute_metrics,  # Optional: Define if you have specific metrics
)

# 11. Minimal Forward Pass Test
# To ensure everything is set up correctly
sample = processed_dataset["train"][0]
pixel_values = sample["pixel_values"].unsqueeze(0).to(device)  # Shape: [1, 3, 224, 224]
labels = sample["labels"].unsqueeze(0).to(device)  # Shape: [1, seq_length]

try:
    outputs = model(pixel_values=pixel_values, labels=labels)
    print("Forward pass successful:", outputs)
except Exception as e:
    print("Error during forward pass:", e)

# 12. Start Training
trainer.train()

Explanation of the Comprehensive Example:

    Dependencies Installation: Ensures all necessary libraries are installed and up-to-date.

    Import Libraries: Imports all required Python libraries.

    CSV Splitting: Splits the labeled CSV into training and validation sets with a 90/10 ratio. If you've already done this, you can skip this step.

    Load Datasets: Loads the split CSVs into Hugging Face Datasets and creates a DatasetDict.

    Initialize Processor and Model: Loads the TrOCRProcessor and VisionEncoderDecoderModel from Hugging Face and moves the model to the appropriate device (GPU/CPU).

    Preprocessing Function: Defines a function to preprocess images and texts, ensuring only pixel_values and labels are returned as lists.

    Apply Preprocessing: Maps the preprocessing function to both training and validation datasets, removing all other columns.

    Set Dataset Format: Explicitly sets the dataset format to PyTorch tensors for the Trainer.

    Define Training Arguments: Sets up Seq2SeqTrainingArguments with W&B disabled to prevent unnecessary prompts.

    Initialize the Trainer: Creates a Seq2SeqTrainer instance with the model, training arguments, and datasets.

    Minimal Forward Pass Test: Performs a minimal forward pass to ensure the model processes the data correctly without errors.

    Start Training: Begins the fine-tuning process.

def preprocess_examples(examples):
    images = []
    texts = []
    for path, text in zip(examples["crop_filename"], examples["recognized_text"]):
        image_path = os.path.join("word_crops", path)
        try:
            img = Image.open(image_path).convert("RGB")
        except Exception as e:

10. Additional Recommendations
A. Define compute_metrics Function

While optional, defining a compute_metrics function can help you monitor training progress using metrics like Character Error Rate (CER) or Word Error Rate (WER).

Example:

from datasets import load_metric

# Load the CER metric
cer_metric = load_metric("cer")

def compute_metrics(eval_pred):
    predictions, labels = eval_pred
    # Decode the predictions and labels
    decoded_preds = processor.batch_decode(predictions, skip_special_tokens=True)
    # Replace -100 in the labels as required by Hugging Face
    labels = [[label for label in label_seq if label != -100] for label_seq in labels]
    decoded_labels = processor.batch_decode(labels, skip_special_tokens=True)
    
    # Compute CER
    cer = cer_metric.compute(predictions=decoded_preds, references=decoded_labels)
    return {"cer": cer}

Update the Trainer:

trainer = Seq2SeqTrainer(
    model=model,
    args=training_args,
    train_dataset=processed_dataset["train"],
    eval_dataset=processed_dataset["validation"],
    tokenizer=processor,
    compute_metrics=compute_metrics  # Add this line
)

B. Monitor GPU Memory

TrOCR models can be memory-intensive. If you encounter CUDA out-of-memory errors:

    Reduce Batch Size: Decrease per_device_train_batch_size and per_device_eval_batch_size in Seq2SeqTrainingArguments.

    Gradient Accumulation: Use gradient accumulation steps to simulate larger batch sizes without increasing memory usage.

    Mixed Precision: Ensure fp16=True if using a GPU that supports it (e.g., NVIDIA RTX series).

C. Save and Load the Fine-Tuned Model

After training, save your fine-tuned model for future use.

# Save the model and processor
model.save_pretrained("trocr-finetuned")
processor.save_pretrained("trocr-finetuned")

To Load Later:

from transformers import TrOCRProcessor, VisionEncoderDecoderModel

processor = TrOCRProcessor.from_pretrained("trocr-finetuned")
model = VisionEncoderDecoderModel.from_pretrained("trocr-finetuned")
model.to(device)

D. Perform Inference with the Fine-Tuned Model

from PIL import Image

# Load an image
image_path = "word_crops/word_0100.png"  # Replace with your image path
image = Image.open(image_path).convert("RGB")

# Preprocess the image
inputs = processor(images=image, return_tensors="pt").pixel_values.to(device)

# Generate predictions
generated_ids = model.generate(inputs)
predicted_text = processor.batch_decode(generated_ids, skip_special_tokens=True)[0]

print("Predicted Text:", predicted_text)

    Preprocessing: Ensure your preprocessing function returns lists (.tolist()) instead of PyTorch tensors. Only include pixel_values and labels.

    Dataset Mapping: Use the remove_columns parameter to exclude all other columns.

    Dataset Format: Explicitly set the dataset format to PyTorch tensors using set_format.

    Training Arguments: Disable W&B by setting report_to="none" in Seq2SeqTrainingArguments.

    Trainer Initialization: Pass the processor correctly and ensure no extra columns are present.

    Verification: Perform a minimal forward pass to confirm the model processes data without errors.

    Start Training: Once all checks pass, initiate the training process.

I'm sorry to hear you're still encountering issues. The error:

TypeError: ViTModel.forward() got an unexpected keyword argument 'num_items_in_batch'

strongly suggests that there's a mismatch between the data being passed to the model and what the model expects. Given that you've identified this as a potential bug in recent library versions, let's explore both workarounds and solutions to get you back on track.
1. Verify and Adjust Library Versions

Recent updates to the transformers or datasets libraries might have introduced changes that are incompatible with your current workflow. Downgrading to a stable version known to work with your code can often resolve such issues.
1.1 Check Current Library Versions

First, determine which versions of transformers and datasets you're currently using:

import transformers
import datasets

print("Transformers version:", transformers.__version__)
print("Datasets version:", datasets.__version__)

1.2 Downgrade to Stable Versions

If your versions are recent (e.g., transformers ≥4.30.0), consider downgrading to versions known to work with your setup. Here's how you can downgrade:

!pip install transformers==4.30.0
!pip install datasets==2.10.1

After running the above commands:

    Restart your Python runtime to ensure changes take effect.
    Re-run your entire script from the beginning to ensure consistency.

Note: Always ensure that downgrading doesn't conflict with other dependencies in your environment.
2. Adjust the Preprocessing Function

The error indicates that unexpected keyword arguments are being passed to the model. This often happens when extra columns are present in your dataset or when the data is incorrectly formatted. Here's how to ensure your preprocessing function is correctly set up:
2.1 Modify the Preprocessing Function to Return Lists

Instead of returning PyTorch tensors directly in the preprocessing function, return standard Python lists. Let the Hugging Face Trainer handle the conversion to tensors.

Updated Preprocessing Function:

from PIL import Image
import os

def preprocess_examples(examples):
    images = []
    texts = []
    for path, text in zip(examples["crop_filename"], examples["recognized_text"]):
        image_path = os.path.join("word_crops", path)  # Adjust if your folder is named differently
        try:
            img = Image.open(image_path).convert("RGB")
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"Error loading image {image_path}: {e}")
            img = Image.new("RGB", (300, 100), color="red")  # Placeholder for missing images
        
        images.append(img)
        texts.append(text)
    
    # Process images and texts without returning tensors
    model_inputs = processor(
        images=images,
        text=texts,
        padding=True,
        truncation=True
    )
    
    # Return the pixel_values and labels as lists
    batch = {
        "pixel_values": model_inputs["pixel_values"],
        "labels": model_inputs["labels"]
    }
    return batch

2.2 Apply the Preprocessing Function and Remove Unnecessary Columns

Ensure that after preprocessing, only the required columns (pixel_values and labels) remain in the dataset.

from datasets import DatasetDict

# Apply the preprocessing to the training set

    preprocess_examples,

)

# Apply the preprocessing to the validation set

)

})

# Verify the columns

print("Validation Dataset Columns:", processed_dataset["validation"].column_names)

Train Dataset Columns: ['pixel_values', 'labels']
Validation Dataset Columns: ['pixel_values', 'labels']

Explicitly set the dataset format to PyTorch tensors. This helps the Trainer understand how to process the data correctly.

    Adjust Preprocessing: Ensure your preprocessing function returns standard Python lists without PyTorch tensors, and only includes the required columns (pixel_values and labels).

I'm sorry to hear you're still encountering issues. The new error message:

Error during forward pass: Make sure to set the decoder_start_token_id attribute of the model's configuration.

indicates that the model's configuration lacks the decoder_start_token_id, which is essential for the decoder to begin generating text sequences. Let's address this step-by-step to resolve the issue and ensure your TrOCR model is correctly fine-tuned using your dataset.
1. Understanding decoder_start_token_id

The decoder_start_token_id is a special token that signals the decoder to start generating the output sequence. Without this token, the decoder doesn't know where to begin, leading to the error you're seeing.
2. Setting decoder_start_token_id in the Model's Configuration

To set the decoder_start_token_id, follow these steps:
2.1. Inspect the Processor's Tokenizer

First, verify what tokens are available in your tokenizer and identify a suitable token to use as the decoder_start_token_id. Typically, this is either the eos_token_id or a specific decoder_start_token_id if defined.

# Inspect tokenizer tokens
print("EOS Token ID:", processor.tokenizer.eos_token_id)
print("Decoder Start Token ID:", processor.tokenizer.decoder_start_token_id)
print("PAD Token ID:", processor.tokenizer.pad_token_id)

Expected Output: You should see integer values corresponding to each token ID. For example:

EOS Token ID: 2
Decoder Start Token ID: None
PAD Token ID: 0

2.2. Assign decoder_start_token_id to the Model's Configuration

If decoder_start_token_id is not set (None), you can assign it to the eos_token_id or another suitable token. Here's how to do it:

# Check and set decoder_start_token_id
if processor.tokenizer.decoder_start_token_id is None:
    # Assign to eos_token_id or another appropriate token
    model.config.decoder_start_token_id = processor.tokenizer.eos_token_id
    print(f"decoder_start_token_id set to eos_token_id: {processor.tokenizer.eos_token_id}")
else:
    model.config.decoder_start_token_id = processor.tokenizer.decoder_start_token_id
    print(f"decoder_start_token_id set to: {processor.tokenizer.decoder_start_token_id}")

Explanation:

    If decoder_start_token_id is None: Assign it to eos_token_id or another appropriate token.
    Else: Use the existing decoder_start_token_id.

Example Output:

decoder_start_token_id set to eos_token_id: 2

2.3. Verify the Configuration

Ensure that the decoder_start_token_id is correctly set in the model's configuration.

print("Model's decoder_start_token_id:", model.config.decoder_start_token_id)

Expected Output:

Model's decoder_start_token_id: 2

3. Correct the Preprocessing Function

Ensure that your preprocessing function returns standard Python lists instead of PyTorch tensors. The datasets library expects data in list-like formats during the mapping process.
3.1. Updated Preprocessing Function

def preprocess_examples(examples):
    images = []
    texts = []
    for path, text in zip(examples["crop_filename"], examples["recognized_text"]):
        image_path = os.path.join("word_crops", path)
        try:
            img = Image.open(image_path).convert("RGB")
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"Error loading image {image_path}: {e}")
            img = Image.new("RGB", (300, 100), color="red")  # Placeholder for missing images
        
        images.append(img)
        texts.append(text)
    
    # Process images and texts without returning tensors
    model_inputs = processor(
        images=images,
        text=texts,
        padding=True,
        truncation=True
    )
    
    # Return the pixel_values and labels as lists
    batch = {
        "pixel_values": model_inputs["pixel_values"],  # These are lists, not tensors
        "labels": model_inputs["labels"]               # These are lists, not tensors
    }
    return batch

Key Points:

    Convert Tensors to Lists: The processor returns tensors when return_tensors="pt" is used. Since we're not using return_tensors, it defaults to returning lists.
    Ensure Only Required Fields Are Returned: Only pixel_values and labels should be returned to prevent any unexpected keyword arguments during the forward pass.

3.2. Apply the Preprocessing Function and Remove Unnecessary Columns

from datasets import DatasetDict

# Apply the preprocessing to the training set
processed_train = dataset["train"].map(
    preprocess_examples,
    batched=True,
    remove_columns=["crop_filename", "recognized_text"]  # Remove original columns
)

# Apply the preprocessing to the validation set
processed_val = dataset["validation"].map(
    preprocess_examples,
    batched=True,
    remove_columns=["crop_filename", "recognized_text"]  # Remove original columns
)

# Create a new DatasetDict with processed datasets
processed_dataset = DatasetDict({
    "train": processed_train,
    "validation": processed_val
})

# Verify the columns
print("Train Dataset Columns:", processed_dataset["train"].column_names)
print("Validation Dataset Columns:", processed_dataset["validation"].column_names)

Expected Output:

Train Dataset Columns: ['pixel_values', 'labels']
Validation Dataset Columns: ['pixel_values', 'labels']

4. Set the Correct Dataset Format

Explicitly set the dataset format to PyTorch tensors. This ensures that the Trainer correctly interprets the data.

processed_dataset.set_format(
    type="torch",
    columns=["pixel_values", "labels"]
)

5. Perform a Minimal Forward Pass Again

Before proceeding with training, perform a minimal forward pass to ensure the model processes the data correctly without errors.

# Select a sample from the training set

# Move inputs to the appropriate device
pixel_values = torch.tensor(sample["pixel_values"]).unsqueeze(0).to(device)  # Shape: [1, 3, 224, 224]

# Perform a forward pass
try:
    outputs = model(pixel_values=pixel_values, labels=labels)
    print("Forward pass successful:", outputs)
except Exception as e:
    print("Error during forward pass:", e)

Expected Behavior:

    No Errors: The forward pass should complete without raising the decoder_start_token_id error.
    Output Object: You should see an output object containing loss and logits.

Sample Output:

Forward pass successful: Seq2SeqLMOutput(loss=..., logits=...)

6. Initialize the Trainer Correctly

Ensure that the Seq2SeqTrainer is correctly set up with the processed datasets and that no extra parameters are being passed inadvertently.
6.1. Define Training Arguments

from transformers import Seq2SeqTrainingArguments

training_args = Seq2SeqTrainingArguments(
    output_dir="./trocr-finetune",
    num_train_epochs=10,
    per_device_train_batch_size=2,
    per_device_eval_batch_size=2,
    evaluation_strategy="steps",
    save_steps=200,
    logging_steps=100,
    eval_steps=200,
    predict_with_generate=True,
    fp16=torch.cuda.is_available(),
    report_to="none",          # Disable Weights & Biases
    logging_dir='./logs',
)

6.2. Define compute_metrics (Optional but Recommended)

Defining a metrics function helps in monitoring training progress. Here's an example using Character Error Rate (CER):

from datasets import load_metric

# Load CER metric
cer_metric = load_metric("cer")

def compute_metrics(eval_pred):
    predictions, labels = eval_pred
    # Decode predictions and labels
    decoded_preds = processor.batch_decode(predictions, skip_special_tokens=True)
    # Replace -100 in the labels as required by Hugging Face
    labels = [[label for label in label_seq if label != -100] for label_seq in labels]
    decoded_labels = processor.batch_decode(labels, skip_special_tokens=True)
    
    # Compute CER
    cer = cer_metric.compute(predictions=decoded_preds, references=decoded_labels)
    return {"cer": cer}

6.3. Initialize the Seq2SeqTrainer

from transformers import Seq2SeqTrainer

trainer = Seq2SeqTrainer(
    model=model,
    args=training_args,
    train_dataset=processed_dataset["train"],
    eval_dataset=processed_dataset["validation"],
    tokenizer=processor,         # Pass the processor which handles both image and text
    compute_metrics=compute_metrics  # Optional: Define if you have specific metrics
)

7. Start Training

With everything set up correctly, initiate the training process.

trainer.train()

Notes:

    Monitor Training Logs: Keep an eye on the logs to ensure training progresses without issues.
    Checkpointing: Models are saved at intervals defined by save_steps. Ensure you have sufficient storage.
